"I am speaking," said a long-winded orator, "for the benefit of posterity." "Yes," said one of his hearers, "and if you keep on much longer your audience will be here." PAPA: "What did you think of the great bicycle race?" Little Son: "Didn't think mach of it." "Everybody said it was wonderful." "I didn't see nothin' wonderful 'bout it. The one that winned couldn't help winning. He leaned over so far forward that he had to go like lightning to keep from fallin' on his nose." JUST HIS HIND.—" We are getting up a concert for the benefit of the church. Won't you take a ticket?" Mrs. Sharpe : "My husband refuses to go to any place where there is to be music." • How lucky! This is just the kind of an enter- tainment he will enjoy." SIGNIFICANT.—First boy: "You're 'fraid to fight, that's what." Second boy "No, I ain't; but if I fight you, my mother'll lick me." UNSELFISH.—Fritz has come home too late and is having a taste of the rod. Fritz : "Oh ! Pa ! Pa! don't over exert yourself; there's Oscar and Hugo still to come !" "How is if," asked a lady, "that Time is always represented as bald-headed?" To which a gentleman replied, "So many have taken Time by the forelock, they probably pulled out all his hair." Miss THIN: "Don't you think my new dress is just exquisite? They all say so." Fannie: 'OII, lovely! I think that dressmaker of yours could make a clothes-prop look graceful." "I've answered thal one more than fifty times this morning." HER FATHER (dubiously) : "I don't know what you are going to marry on. You have but a small salary, and my daughter has no fortune." Her Lover (confidently) : "Oh, well, if you are econo- mical for a year or two you'll be able to start us all right." JUDGE: "And what did the prisoner say when you told him that you would have him arrested ?," Complainant: "He answered me mechanically, .you- honour." Judge: "Explain." Complainant: "He hit me on the head with a hammer." HUSBAND: "Do you know that every time a woman gets angry she adds a new wrinkle to her face?" Wife: "No, I did not; but if it is so, I presume it is a wise provision of nature to let the world know what sort of a husband the woman has."

To RUSSIA FOR WARMTH. —Five years ago Lord -Randolph Churchill undertook a journey to Russia. A friend asked him what he had gone there for. "To get warm !" was the reply. And he went on to explain the apparent paradox. "The winter is very cold everywhere, but it is much colder in Russia than anywhere else. Hence the Russians take the most efficient precautions against its rigour. It is only in St. Petersburg that people understand the art of heating a house thoroughly." As old lady from the country was at one of the large London stations, when she noticed a carriage labelled "Engaged." It happened that in the said carriage were a young lady and a gentleman, who appeared to be on the best terms with one another. The old lady remarked that anyone could see they were engaged, without having the fact placarded to the world. A GENTLEMAN in a restaurant got a fish-bone across his throat, and was very nearly choked. A waiter rendered him a little assistiuce by loosen- ,.ing his collar and giving him a glass of water. When the gentleman received his bill he was dis- gusted to find that a shilling had been charged for this slight service. "What is the meaning of this, waiter?" he asked, angrily, pointing to the offending item. The waiter looked at it and re- plied: "Well, sir, choking's a hextra ! A STRANGER in London the other day, going to the City, asked a casual passer-by if he could direct him to New Broad-street. The fellow thought for s while, and then said he did not know, anti proceeded on his way. Before the stranger had gone fifty yards farther he heard footsteps running in his direction, and looking back he perceived the man he had before ad. dressed, who, fearfully out of breath, rushed up to him and exclaimed : "Perhaps it is Old Broad. street you want?" "Well, where's that?' in- quired the stranger, when to his surprise the leilow answered : " I don't know where that is, either."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 11. 1895

No fewer than seven hundred and fifty thousand sew ing machines are made and sold every year by a single great British sewing-machine c'mpany. The King of Siam has a bodyguard of female warriors. It is composed of four hundred girls, chosen from amongst the strongest and most handsome of all the ladies in the land. At the age of thirteen they enter the service, and remain in it till they are twenty-five, when they pass into the reserve. Their weapon is the lance.

. - A gentleman of an inquiring turn of mind has been testing the honesty of the people by dropping in the streets and on the floors of big drapers' shops six ladies' purses, each containing one shilling and ninepence in varied change, a key, and a Card with his name and address thereon. Five of the purses were returned in less than twenty-four hours, and in each case the finders were women. This appears to demonstrate that lost purses are likely to be restored to the owners when they do not contain more than one shilling and ninepence, and do not happen to be picked up by men. In the Ukraine, Russia, the woman does the courting. When she falls in love she goes to the man's house and tells him the state of her feelings, and if he does not agree to her proposals she remains there hoping to c -ax him into accepting her. The man cannot treat her with the least discourtesy, or turn her out, for her friends would be sure to avenge the insult ; so, if he wants to escape her, his only chance is to leave the house and re- main away until she is gone. A Swiss doctor has counted by pedometer the number of his footsteps during the year. They come to nine million seven hundred and sixty thousand,an average of twenty-six thousand seven hundred and forty daily. Of the whole,from six hundred thousand to seven hundred thousand steps were taken up and down staircases, or from one thousand five hundred to two thousand a day. Reckoning two 'steps a second,and two metres for three steps, the distance covered is more than seventeen kilo- metres—say, ten miles a day. It is to be concluded that the doctor does not keep a brougham, and has a large practice.

Singular Domestic Arrangements. One of Professor Edison's managers has a house, which, thanks to his electrical knowledge, is exceedingly startling to the casual visitor. As one approaches the residence, the front gate swings open and shuts auto- matically. The visitor's foot, pressing on the doorstep, rings a bell in the kitchen and one in the master's study, and by touching a button, the front door is opened before there is time to knock. Musical boxes play when the guests sit down on chairs or sofas. The cruets run about the dinner-table on a miniature electric line, and even when the visitor retires to his or her chamber, the "Spinning Wheel" tells us, the bed unfelds itself by electricity.

A Lady Sculptor. Miss Alice M. Chaplin, the eminent lady-sculptor, has been honoured on various occasions during the last four- teen years with commissions from the Queen. Her first commission for Her Majesty was a statuette of "Flora," the Queen's favourite collie. She executed it at Buck- ingham Palace in terra-cAtte, and it was afterwards pro- duced in bronze and oxidised silver. Miss Chaplin's specialite is animals; she has exhibited some of her groups at the Royal Academy, and is a passionate lover of all animals. In a chat with a recent interviewer, she says: "As a young girl, when I made pastry, I used to adorn my handiwork with birds and beasts in pdste. One day I bought a piece of clay, and when my domes- tic duties were over, I tied the cat up in an arm-chair and tried to take her portrait. I worked at that for six weeks. I've never had time to labour on anything else as I did on that. I bad not tools, and it was finished I with a pin."

Shoes of the World. The Portuguese shoe has a wooden sole and heel, with a vamp made of patent leather, fancifully showing the flesh side of the skin. The Persian footgear is a raised shoe, and is often a foot high. It is made of light wood richly inlaid, with a strap extending over the instep. The Armenian shoe has a leather sole and heel,without a counter and back quarter. The vamp is made of felt, and is beautifully ornamented with needlework done in coloured silk thread. The Muscovite shoe is hand-woven on a wooden frame and but little attention is paid to the shape of the foot. Leather is sometimes used, but the sandal is generally made of silk coloured cordage and woollen cloth. The Siam shoe has the form of an ancient canoe, with a gondola bow and an open top. The sole 311 made of wood, and the upper of inlaid wood and cloth, and the exterior is elaborately ornamented in colours and with gold and silver. The sandal worn by the Egyptians is comnosed of a sole made by sticking together three thicknesses ot leather. This is held to the foot by passing a band across the instep. The sandal is beautifully stitched with threads of different colours. The Grecian shoe is made almost entirely of leather, and has a thickly padded sole with a sharp turn-up toe, which is surmounted by a large ball of coloured wool or hair. The shoe is fleece-lined, and is gorgeously deco- rated with beads and ornamental stitching. The Hungarian shoe,or mocassin, is made of raw hide, prepared by a sun curing process. It is bound together with many thongs of raw hide. Loops, or thongs, ex- tend upwards round the ankle, and through these loops is passed a strap which is buckled at the side. The clog worn by the Japanese is of wood, and as viewed from the side is the shape of a boy's sledge. It is fastened to the foot by a string, which passes between the great and the second tee, and across the former. A strap, an inch in width and lined with linen, is carried across the instep.

ofwtrp,4)its he joined in the fr,iy, and very 1 • e became the leader, at ti acting to hdt "IA spirits of the crew, as the nia piet attracts points f mteel. On the instant he was train-formed into . rery god of war, and all the grand qualities 01 In "nand which had been alumbering within Min inalities inherited from his father and Lorn with him—were aroused into action. With a physic strength which was a native quality of his magniti- ent frame; with an endurance which had keen oegotten of long training in manly sports ; with a will which was firmness itself when fixed in the right ; and with a courage which knew not the thought of fear, he advanced against the foemen, and struck blows which counted fearfully every time. The pirate chieftain was a powerful, dark-faced man, with a voice like a whirlwind, and an arm of immense strength. He had cut his way to the cabin door, and had there turned, when Arthur met him. In a moment their swords were crossed, and for the first time during the fray our hero had opportunity to hilly test the skin which the most approved study and practice had given him. The heavy cutlass of the buccaneer was wielded with wondrous power, but the subtle play of the Eng- lish youth put him wholly at faalt. "That for you cried the swart chieftain, aim- ing a blow at the head of his antagonist which ha seemed to think must be irresistible. But his eye had given sufficient key to his intent even before his weapon had been raised, and Arthur, instead of attempting to parry, leaped nimbly aside, drawing his sword-arm well back, and on the next instant his keen blade was driven clean through the pirate's body. Our hero had just shaken the falling chief from hie sabre when his eye caught a scene by one of the earronades that made him start, and with a quick my of alarm,

he leaped forward.' In order that the reader may understand the situation we must go back a brief space in the battle. Very shortly after the conflict bad com- menced Sir jasper found that the old wound in his shoulder had so far wrought upon his right arm as to utterly preclude the possibility of his using his sword, and when he had discharged his pistols' he fell back to re-load them. He had charged one of them when he was forced to change his position, and as he caught sight of Ar- thur Rainsford pressing forward to the lead and marked the effect which his heroism had upon the men, he felt confident that the Britons would win the day and that the pirates must be driven back to their own vessel. He had looked upon the deck of the brig and observed that only one man, able to wield a weapon, had been left in charge after the grapplings had been secured, and the thought )ad struck him that he. could not do better with khe strength that was his than to slip down and disable the long brass gun ; for he knew very well wnat mischief a vanquished foe might work with it. Directly under the break of the poop was a. tool-chest, and from this he took a small rattail file and a hammer, and having thrust them into his pocket he slipped out through the after-port down upon the deck of the brig. With his re- loaded pistol he shot the surprised sentinel through the head, and then hastening to the brass gun he. drove the slender file into the priming-vent as far as he could, and broke it off, after which, with one ,or two smart blows, he settled the part remaining in the vent below the surrounding service of the piece. 'This done he made the best of his way back to the deck of the ship; and as he entered at the open port he found himself confronted by two of the pirates. Whether they were on the point of retreating to the brig, or whether they had witnessed his work of spiking their gun, he could not determine; but they instantly recognized him as one of the chiefs of the ship's force, and made ready to slay him. The old soldier still held the hammer in his hand, and with this he sought to defend himself ; but his arm was weak, and he was borne back over the carronade, one of the pirates holding him in that position,. while the other raised his cutlass to run hiin through. This was the situation which had caught Ar- thur's eye, and which he had hastened to cireuna- vent. A reeling pirate who stood in his way he pushed aside as lie would have removed a child from his path, and on the next instant he was at the breech of the gun. Sir Jasper had been borne back supinely, without the power to help himself, and when he saw the second villain draw back his blade for the deadly thrust he believed the end was at hand, and of the swiftly varying thoughts that flashed through his mind during the brief suspense the uttermost, and those which coloured all the rest, were of Mary. He had murmured her name in a fervent prayer, and was wondering what should be the character of his next awakening, when something flashed like a stream of light across the line of his vision—he heard a heavy thud—and the man who was aiming at his life sank from his sight as though he had been stricken by a bolt from heaven. Another flash, and he who had held him down upon the gun likewise fell. "Sir Jasper 1 God grant that harm hath not befallen you I" He was raised up by a strong hand, and stood face to face with his preserver. " Rainsford - "You are not harmed, Sir Jasper?" "Thank God, no !" "Then rest you here. This work will soon bo

done." Thus speaking, the youthful hero once more faced the foe, hut there was little fighting to be done. The pirates had lost their chief officers, and as their numbers grew less and less, they became utterly disheartened and demoralised, and fell back from the fray. A few who had been forced to the waist leaped over the gangway upon the deck of their own vessel, snd the others, when they saw this, followed as swiftly as possible. Some of the more fiery of the English crew, fraught with the spirit of vengeance, would have given pursuit, but Arthur restrained them. "If they will go," he cried, "let them go, in God's name! Blood enough has been shed. We have the ship to save." And he thought of another thing to be saved— something near which he would have deadly com- bat no longer than was necessary. In a very few minutes from the retreat of the first of the vanquished pirates all who were alive of their crew had left the deck of the Indiaman. A few of the wounded ones were helped over the side by the visitors, and shortly afterwards the grapplings were cast off and the ship's main-top- sail was given to the breeze. As she began to gather headway Capt. Harlow looked back upoa the deck of the brig, and saw that the pirates were loading their long gun. "My God 1" he exclaimed," we are not free yet. If they should cripple us, they might lie off and sink us." "Be under no apprehension, Captain," said Sir Jasper, with a look of grim satisfaction. " They wont fire that gun again until they have drilled a new priming vent." "Sir Jasper," cried Arthur, with a gleam of in- telligence upon his face, "I saw you coming in at the port." "Yes," returned the old soldier, "I had fore- seen this very emergency, and had been down to spike their gun, and I can assure you that it is spiked beyond all their power to free it." And his words proved true. The pirates were seen at work upon the breech of their gun while the burning match stood unused in its tub. They punched with the priming-wire, and they pounded with a hammer, and without doubt they swore a great many fearful oaths; but all to no effect. They were left farther and farther astern, and upon the redeemed Indiaman they could do no more harm. Redeemed, indeed, had been the good old ship, but at a fearful cost. Some of the beat and the bravest of her crew had fallen ; but they had fallen right loyally, and the crown of glorious victory descended upon them from the result of their work. True and loving hearts mourned for , them and sincere prayers in their behalf —prayers from lips that spoke with earnest faith—were I wafted to the throne of the Eternal Father as the blue waters closed above their ocean graves. LADIES' LETTER.

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

I shall begin my weekly outpouring with a description of a few of the elegant Paris fashione now being worn in the country and at the seaside. Here is a bright pink satin antique. It has a plain skirt five yards round, the bodice well draped, and large elbow sleeves, the bodice having bretelles at the back, and a yoke of black velvet, embroidered with steel, from which falls a steel fringe, forming three points over white lace. A dress of apple- green linen, with ten rows of Valenciennes inser- tion going round the skirt, over silk, the same shade of green : linen chemisette boullante, with a black bow on the left shoulder, and a deep berthe of Valenciennes: the sleeves also striped with Valenciennes, very full to the elbow; bows and band of black velvet striped with green. • -

A pretty blouse is of pink Chine silk, with a blurred design in cerise, and the little rosettes on the sleeves and the folded collar and bows are all of surah in a deep cerise tint. Down the front and back of the blouse is a wide box-pleat, and this at the front has insertion lace, nearly covering the pleat, and a couple of paste buttons. The sleeves are short, in the now fashionable style, and on warm days the blouse is wearable without the sleeveless jacket, and on cool days a more sub- stantial bodice or blouse will quite alter the appearance of the cbAtume. A very similar blouse is made in blue Clone silk, with insertion and narrow frills of lace across the bodice. The novel sleeves are gored to the wrist, and piped with Valenciennes lace, a most stylish arrangement.

A stylish skirt and sleeveless bodice with two or three blouses, will be quite sufficient to ensure a stylish appearance to its possessor during a holiday of two or three weeks, in addition, of course, to the coat and skirt travelling dress, and this also may be varied, if necessary, by a second smart little coat made of material to match the skirt. The short Eton jacket is quite separate, and the collar removable, and when the jacket is worn with a sailor hat, a delightfully nautical effect may be obtained by donning a wide sailor collar and cuffs of white drill, edged with blue, and adding a sleeveless vest of blue and white drill, in narrow stripes. With carefully 3hoBen accessories of this description, it is wonder- ful how the costume may be changed, and a blue serge arrangad as described, and with tan boots, gloves, and stockings, and a white sunshade, forms an ideal toilette for the sea-shore; or, with a sleeveless front of pleated silk, and a lawn collar Ind cuffs, trimmed with lace, as shown in the sketch, will have a smart, dressy effect, and can be worn with the black hat adorned with roses. The " cross-over " blouses are also very popular. A wide lace collar is an ornament to these costumes.

I saw last week a very pretty evening gown made in yellow satin, the sleeves covered with purple chiffon. It was as most of the best dresses are now, full and simple in the skirt, having just a bow of velvet in front at the hem, intermixed flowers, a trimming which was repeated at the waist of the pretty bodice. This was richly em- broidered with turquoise and pearls; a triple fes- toon of pearls in front, a bow of ribbon on one shoulder, and a bunch of flowers on the other. The cut of these sleeves, with double puffs, large and small, the large at the top, is a mode which is likely to linger with us.

Here are a couple of hats, or rather, a toque and a hat. The first is well adapted to the season and coming harvest, and is daring in its mixture of colour. The bows are o: rose-coloured velvet in- termingled with green and yellow corn and poppies, the bows and the bearded grain blending well to- gether, while the poppies droop becomingly on the hair. Toques are likely to remain some time in fashion. it would I,..lifficult to find a more becom- ing hat to an English lace than this one of straw. Though broad in ti.e brim, it does not overshadow the face, but widens at the sides. Outside it la covered with marguerites, brought into greater pro- minence by bows and green velvet, a spray of flowers standing out boldly at the side. The crown is somewhat high, and widens towards the top; a double set of white wings match the white hat. Then there is another in light brown fancy straw. This has two rosettes or r6siSila velvet and a wreath of cornflowers and smart bow of shot blue and brown ribbon.

Your loving JENNIA.

MARK OF THE BLACK CROSS. BY BYLVANUS COBB, SS.

CHAPTER vim 'THE OCEAN BATTLE! The brig had so far proved her sailing qualities that all hope of escape had been for some time given up by the Indiaman's crew; yet, from an Instinct ever present with a chase, they had stood on while they could, ready to accept any accident that might fall in their favour. No such accident occurred, however; and when the pirate com- menced to discharge his long gun within effective range Capt. Harlow called his officers together for consultation, deferring to Sir Jasper in such matters as he was willing to consider. "My own opinion is," said Harlow, "that we had better heave. to. It is painful and humiliating, His speech was cut short by the tearing of a chain shot through the leech of the spanker. "That answers the question," said Sir Jasper. "Our rigging is at their mercy, and if we leave it for them to heave us to, they will do it with a vengeance. I think the quicker you come-to, the better. Without further delay the mainsail was clewed up, and the main-topsail laid to the mast. The ship now lay with her head to the southward and east- ward, with her larboard tack aboard, while the pirate was a mile and a half distant to leeward, standing very near north, upon the starboard tack. "Now," said Sir Jasper, as soon as the braces had been belayed, "the next thing for us to do, is to fix our battery." "Will you set the guns Sir Jasper ?" "If I were upon the field, Captain, I should do so with some degreee of confidence; but in this instance I must defer to your better judgment. Only I may make this remark: Let your guns be so set that they can rake the rascal, if possible. How will the pirate come up? Of course he will run us by the board ?" "I think so," replied Harlow. "It is plain to be seen that the brig is handled by an experienced sailor; and I can only judge what I would do if I were in his place. By standing on upon his present course, and hugging the wind, as he seems Inclined to do since we hove-to, he will run under our stern not more than two cable's-length away; and when at a proper distance he oan go about and drop down upon our weather quarter." "And this you think he will do ?" "Yes. I shoulti do it if I were in his place." "Then let us govern ourselves accordingly, and mask all four of our guns on the larboard quarter." Agreeably to this plan the four carronades were set upon the larboard side of the quarter-deck, where there were just ports enough to accommodate them. These port-holes were covered by hanging screens, which upon being unhooked could be thrown up by the running out of the guns, so that, as the bul- warks were high, the enemy could have no idea of armament until the muzzles wee exposed. The guns were heavily loaded with scrap-iron—terrible missiles for short range—and then secured by breeching and side-tacktes, and Sir .Jasper himself, at the earnest request of his companions, assumed command of the battery. Meantime the stnaii-arms had been loaded and distributed, and the crew instructed in the tactics they might be called upon to follow. There were still to be seen pale faces and quak- ing limbs, but nothing that looked like failing or faltering. At length the pirate came up under the stern, at about the distance which Harlow had antici- pated, and when she had gained a favourable posi- tion to windward she went about, and clued up her courses, prepared to drop clown upon the India- man's weather beam. Iler crew, a motley horde, armed with pistols and cutlasses and knives, crowded upon the lee rail, presenting an aspect bloodthirsty and terrible. At this juncture, when the pirate's course had been fully determined, Mary Wallace was per- suaded to retire. When she passed Arthur, who stood by one of the carronades, she touched him upon the arm, and said to him: If the fervent prayers of a contrite and trust- ing spirit can avail anything in this terrible hour, know that there is one who will not forget to pray, if she may not fight." "God bless and save you I" ejaculated Arthur. And with these words he took her hand and led her to the hatchway. He would have said more, but he dared not trust himself. Even amid the mutterings of that dreadful danger, with the storm of battle gathering about him, his heart had gone forth to that beautiful girl, with its full and undi- vided burden of love; and he had become a devoted worshipper at the shrine of her purity and loveliness. And the presence of the grim pirate, with death threatening in a hundred shapes, was not sufficient to prevent him from picturing to him- self the bright and blessed future which might be his if only SHE could share it with him. Had Sir Jasper been given the privilege to plaoe the brig as beat suited his purpose he could not have selected for her a position under his batter), more favourable than that which she assumed of her own accord. She had gone about upon the ship's weather quarter, and was dropping alongside with just enough wind in her sails to bring her to the re(p, position. The old soldier had seen his guns properly de- pressed and trained, and the lighted matches were in the hands of trusty men, who understood well their duties. "Brie ahoy 1" shouted Captain Harlow, from the horse-block. He did not care to expose himself upon the poop., "Aye! aye ! was returned from the brig. "What brig is that?" The answer was the running up of a black flag to the brig's peak, in the centre of the dark field of which was the ghostly device of a pair of crossed- bones surmounted by a human skull. The showing of that bloody flag at that time was evidently in- tended to inspire terror on the part of the crew of the ship. "What ship is that?" demanded the pirate, with a strong Portuguese accent. The answer of the Indiaman was one for which the rascals were evidently not prepared. They were crowding up the rail of their vessel, ready for a spring into the chains of the ship, some with their pistols, and some with their cutlasses, ready for use. Gen. Wallace saw that the moment had come, and he gave the order for running out the guns. The movement was executed without mishap, and it was found that the two after guns bore directly upon the brig's crowded deck. "Numbers One and Two !—Fire The matches were applied, and the carronades belched forth two dense volumes of fire and smoke, and amid the roar could be heard the tearing and crashing of the iron hail upon the pirate's deck. "Numbers Three and Four/—FIRE I" By the brig's top-hamper above the smoke Sir Jasper could see that she had ranged up within reach of the other two guns' and they were dis- charged together ; and from the howling and the screeching which arose above all other din it was evident that they had done fearful execution. But the British crew knew that the survivors of the piratical horde would only be made more desperate by this blow, and they nerved themselves accordingly. Still, the havoc made by the ragged missiles with which the carronades had been loaded had greatly lessened the invading force, and had, furthermore thrown into confusion those who were prepared to board. The result was that the pirates came tearing wildly up the main and mizzen rigging, where they were met by the steady fire of the musketeers, joined by a score of pistols. As it was, a score of the freebooters gained the ship's deck, and as they leaped impetuously from the netting': the Englishmen were borne back upon the opposite side. More of the pirates soon came pouring in, and when Sir Jasper had had oppor- tunity to take in the whole situation he found that the enemy did not greatly outnumber the British force. Full two-score of the rascals had been either killed or disabled by the fire of the carronades, and at least half a score had been shot down while coming over the side. True it is that in a battle confined within limits so narrow as those of the deck of a ship men will take the guiding instinct from him who is foremost in the fight, and the more especially so when that foremost man is one whose moral force is equal to his physical display. Now Arthur Rainsford, when the battle com- menced, had no thought of leading the fight. He had thought to look to Sir Jaser and to Captain Harlow, and to follow in their 'lead; but, further than this—or deeper than this—he had been moved by the thought of saving Mary Wallace from a fate which none on earth could be more horrible. And If he would save her, he must overcome the pirates nd diive them back to their vessels. With such

A MINER'S WIFE TORTURED.

We recently had occasion (writes a " Wakefield Ex- press reporter) to visit the coal mining district of New Saarlston, and at No. 30, Crossley-street. Sharlston, met Mrs. Thompson, wife of a miner, who told an extraor- dinary tale of sufferings. About 14 years ago Mrs. Thompson contracted a severe cold,whieh rapidly developed into rheumatism of the most acute kind. She was known all over the neigh- bourhood as one of the greatest sufferers in England; un- able to move without aid, and tormented day and night by the most poignant anguish of body and limb. She had the greatest difficulty in moving about the house with two sticks or crutches. Her apprthe failed, and she became a mere shadow of her former self,her weight falling off to the extent of nearly three stone. Mrs. Thompson's condition became serious, and much sym- pathy was elicited on her behalf, but even the skill of the best local doctors had not the desired effect. At a heavy expense the bath treatment was tried in succes- sion at Buxton, Matlock, Askern, and Harrogate, —and all in vain. The tortures continued, and Mrs. Thomp- son began to despair, and to think that her case was hopeless or incurable. One day Mrs. Thompson purchased a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pate People, a remedy not like other medicine, which has cured some very obstinate cases. From the time she commenced to take them she gradually improved. To-day she is in far better health than she has been for the last thirteen years. No doubt there are certain people (says the " Wakefield Express ") who will designate this" an advertising puff," but we can assure our readers on the authority of our reporter, and on the evidence of many of the residents in Sharlston, that the above statement is literally true and not exaggerated. Mrs. Thompson said ?` I feel thankful that I was ever led by the good hand of God to try this marvellous remedy. I have continued taking the Pills, my appetite has improved. my weight has gone op from six stone ten pounds to about nine stone." THE ELBE DISASTER.

-- The final deliverance of the German Admiralty Court en the collision between the Elbe and the Crathie is pre- cisely what we anticipated. The Court found that the officer of the watch on the Crathie was in fault for not keeping a proper hr k-out, that the offie.er who was in charge of the Elbe might have prevented the collision if he had taken proper steps, and that the master of the Crathie was not te blame for his " omitted attempts " to save the passeDgers and crew of the Elbe, becau,e his own vessel was in such a condition that he was justified in believinr, she was about te founder. The Court might have gone further and admitted that it was not pranible for th Ctathie to have rescued any of the un- fortunate people frrm the Elbe. The British steamer was turned round as soon as it had been ascertained that she wruld float, but by that time the Elbe had run a nsideratle distance and sank before the Crathie could get near the spot. Indeed the fact that the Elbe had I gone down was not known to the master of the Crathie until he had got into a German port. The officer of the Crathie was no doubt, on the finding of the Court, primarily to blame for the overwhelming disaster, but the fact remains that the Elbe—which had twice the speed of the Crathie—could have got out of the way if the officer in command had chosen to go out of his course a mile or two.,

The frog barometer, used in Germany and Switzer- land, is a very simple apparatus, consisting of a jar of , water, a frog and a little wooden step-ladder. If the frog comes out and sits on the steps, rain is expected.

"CAPITAL" CHAT

French journals have discovered a flea evidence of the better understanding between Germany and Great Britain in the presence of British warships off Tangier. This time they are pretty nearly right, as our ships are sent to back up a German claim for redress. The murder of a second German merchant in Morocco made matters rather serious, and the pressure of the combined fleets has had the desired effect. Germany will be grateful enough to assist Great Britain elsewhere, most likely in Egypt, when that matter becomes acute, as it most cer- tainly wilt do before long. There is a suspicion that Lord alisbury does not mean to evacuate Egypt at all, and that the acrimony of France as well as the attitude of the Sultan relative to the Armenian reforms is due to strong suspicions on this peint.

Alehough Somerset House is well aware that tome efforts have been and are being made to evade the new estate duty, there is no fear that they will be serioug enough to affect revenue or to necessitate legislation in the way of prevention, or even of repeal. A pretty sharp look-out is kept over these matters, and no systematic irregularities could eccur without detection. It will be time enough to discuss remedies when there is a real evil to be dealt with, and no indications of such an evil have been manifest. The impression that the present Government will boldly propose repeal rests upon no solid foundation.

Mr. Gully, the new speaker, is the only member of Parliament entitled to be well satisfied with Mon- day's proceedings. It was rather dull for many others, apart from the cheerful talk on the flier of the HOll9B before the election ot Speaker began, and the after-glow of gossip in the lobby. Members attended early, in some cases strolled in uncon- cernedly, in others to look pleased, indifferent. or hopeful, secured good seats or none at all, cheered their leaders, did their duty, and then separated. A glance at the majority, densely packed, shows the, not many of the new members are very young. A look of middle-age, solidity, and sobriety is obvious everywhere. Evidently it will be a safe party, easily led, not given to revolt, and only inlined to jib when extreme measures are proposed. It is a party that will look to the Ministerial bench for ideas, and cheer itcelf hoarse on slight provocation. On the Opposition side some of the new 'members look very capable, earnest men, who will not begin to try their strength too early. When Mr. Balfour wished the Speaker' a period of calm and peace " the Opposition laughed without any sting in its merriment, and Mr. Balfour's eyes twinkled as if he also saw the humour of the suggestion.

Anybody know anything of " Mr. Whittington Barnes "1 asks the " Realm." The London corres- pondent of the " Echo de Paris " has interviewed Mr. Barnes, and says that he is the President of the Thames Valley Legitimist Club, and that no fewer than three thousand politicians share Mr. Barnes's notions of the legitimate. According to these notions. anarchy has been the condition of England ' since 1688,when o.tr last legitimate sovereign evacu- ated the country. No Act of Parliament since that event has been valid. There is no such thing as the Hanoverian Succession, and her present Majesty Mr. Barnes grieves to say, is an usurper. The lady who should be resident at Buckingham Palace, it appears, ascended the English throne, in exile, in 1875. She died in 1878, leaving the crown to her son Robert, Prince of Wales,the only genuine. The Prince is, at present, somewhere in Bavaria, and we are governed by a Council of Regency during his minority. At the latter end of next year, however, the Prince will come of age, and will be proclaimed sing of England., France and Ireland. Mr. Barnes will thus, besides providing us with a king we can do without, burden the Republic over the way with yet another king it cannot do with. When Mr. Balfour congratulated the burly mem- ber for Gateshead on his return to the House of Commons, Mr. Allen said that "when the Liberal ship was wrecked on the Tory rocks he swam ashore by the aid of a plank—a good Scotch ,fir," he added with a twinkle in his eye. After shaking hands with Mr. Chamberlain, Mr. Allen said to him. "And now you've got the power, I hope you'll give us some of the good social measures you talked about in your younger days."

Committee-room No. 15 was on Tuesday the scene ot quite a harmonious gathering, after There was none of the tumult that bad been pre- dicted. "I have never in my life attended a more peaceable meeting of our party," was the remark one of the oldest Nationalist members made to me when it was all over. Mr. Justin M'Carthi, was unanimously elected chairman. The ,question of dispensing with a committee was raised by Mr. Bealy's friends, in order that the party as a whole should manage its affairs, but it was defeated by a majority of nearly 2 to I. The election of the committee was 'deferred until Thursday. Dr. Tanner, Captain Dmelan, and Mr. Donal Sullivan were chosen as Whips.

It is of interest to hear that a large contract has recently been arranged between one of the leading Arms in Berlin and a prominent English company for the supply of the latter to Germany of " mi- trailleuses," to the value of nearly a quarter of a million sterling. The guns, which will be the Maxim principle,are due to be delivered in January next, and there is every likelihood that other similar contracts will be placNl with the same firm. It may be added that the company with which the contracts have been placed in England has at least three or four years' work in hand for the German and other Continental Governments.

A very proper and determined attack has begun on the cyclists in London streets,who think if they ring a bell they cease to be responsible for any accidents they may cause. The fines inflicted in the police courts during the last few days,especially in the City proper, have been most useful in pro- tecting the rights of the public, who are liable to be run into by cyclists under circumstances which render escape next to impossible. It is not im- probable that cycling for pleasure in London streets will ultimately have to be put down. It is getting to be a great danger to everybody.

I find that, in 1878, the year after Cleopatra's Needle was erected, it was dressel with some in- durating chemical solution, and the men then worked from a scaffold, which was very costly to erect. This time American ladders were suggested, but it was intimated that the workmen would be exposed to considerable risk in using them. Even- tually, the plan of using the hose was propounded, which has now, without any mishap, been effectu- ally carried nut. The Needle is looking fresh and bright.

Nothing is yet officially .known as to when the Shalizada will begin his homeward journey, but it is certain that his departure will not be made the subject of any demonstration. lam informed that the Government officials are perplexed about him. The £l,OOO a week still continues to be paid for Dorchester House. The India Office has refused to pay for his continental trip, which trip is the less necessary as the Sultan refuses to see him, and his father the Ameer tins written angry letters about the proposed journey to Paris, Vienna, and the Golden Horn, and urges his return.

Probably no cricket match this season has aroused quite so much interest as that which ended on Wednesday afternoon so disastrously for the champion county. On Monday the visitors at, Kennington Oval numbered nearly 20; 00; and on Wednesday, after a blank day on Tuesday, over 12,000 persons passed through the turnstiles. As the match was a benefit for Hobert Abel, one of the best and worthiest professional players in the country, it is only to be regretted that there should not have been a larger "gate" on each of the three days, but as it is he will no doubt reap a handsothe reward tor his long and faithful service to Surrey. The game was throughout a triumph for Yorkshire, who never gave the home side a chance, and the result will afford them material consolation for two defeats last year and one in the early part of the present season at the hands of the Surrey team. The all-round play of the Yorkshiremen was simply admirable; Brown's punishing hitting on Monday afternoon, the bowling of Hirst and Peel and Wain- wright, and the fielding of the whole side being equally worthy of praise. The Surrey men, on the other hand, quite failed to rise to the occasion. The truth is, they play a very poor losing game. Once let them get the upper hand and they are invin- cible; but tp-day, as in the disappointing match against" England ' earlier in the year, they stiepf,d when once Collare‘l like a beaten Ride, fight at all.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 17 1895.

NEW BOOKSIMA.GAZINES,ETC.

MACMILLAN AND Co.—Now that the new Parlia- ment has been formed and a majority of its mem- bers are strangers to the country, the shilling edi- tion by these publishers of " The New House of Commons," with lliographical notices of its mem- bers cannot be anything but highly acceptable. Not only are the biographies of its members given, but of each constituency are given the recorded polls comparisons with previous elections, and also stati- stiaal analysis of elections. The whole information is from the" Times," and another point in the favour of the book is the admirable system of ar- rangement adopted in dealing with the matter, making reference to the multifarious imformation an exceedingly simple task.—The second volume of the Pocket Edition of Charles Kingsley's works is embodied in" Alton Locke," to winch an interest- ing prefatory memoir is given by Thomas Hughes, QC., (the author of "Tom Browne's Schooldays "). This edition,with its many merits, is certain to ob- tain popularity, combining, as it does, the neces- sary qualifications,of cheapness and excellence.-- Another series, which received and still receives a hearty welcome—the Illustrated Standard Novels Series—is getting well advanced, the 611 and 7th editions having been issued. Of these two the first to appear was the one containing " Maid Marian," and" Crotchet Castle," by Thomas Love Peacock, with admirable illustraticns by Mr. F. H. Town- send; indeed the faithfulness and general excel- lence of the illustrations in all the numbers of this series have been very favourably remarked upon,as these are points not alwa3 if duly recognised in simi- lar productions. In a prolificiiintreduction Mr. George Saintsbury mentions that " Maid Marian " came out in 1822, and was very popular both in book form and as the basis of an operetta 'which Planche founded on it, and to which Sir Henry Bishop sup- plied the music. " Crotchet Castle," which Mr. Saintsbury describes as by general consent the completest and urbanest product of the author's genius," was published in 1831. The second of the two books mentioned is " Ormond," a tale by Maria Edgworth, and characteristically illustrated by Carl Schloesser, while the natural attraction of the story is supplemented by an introduction from the pen of Ann Thackeray Ritchie, who describes " Ormond "as one of the " most spontaneous" of Miss Edgworth's books. It was published in 1817 and written during the last illness of the authoress's father.—ln the Englisn Men of Action Series, "Nel- son,"by John Knox Laughton will take a prominent place,notwithstanding that the great sea-hero's bio- graphy has so often previously been placed before the public. Mr. Laughton follows out Nelson's career with evident love of detail, and displays an intimate acquaintanceship with his actions and their " cause and effect." SIMPICIN, MARSHALL, HAMILTON, KENT, AND CO.--" A shilling book on Roses and Rose Culture," by William Paul, F.L.S.R. H.S., provides all the theoretical knowledge necessary for a practical and successful culture of roses under varied conditions. Soils and climate are dealt with and instruction • given as regards pruning, hybridising, raising 'seedlings, and in fact upon everything, In his pre- face the author describes his book as" the rationale of cultivation in a nutshell. CASSELL AND Ca—The official guides to the Great Eastern and London and North-Western Railways have been issued by these publishers at the price of 3d. each. The map of each line is given and the different towns on the routes of travel are described in an interesting manner. Neat illustra- tions are scattered through the guides.—Cassell's Saturday Journal for August has an additional 16 page pictorial.supplement illustrating moving in- cidents in the life of Her Majesty. the Queen, with descriptive text. Apart from this there is the usual budget of complete stories, humorous pictures, serials, and attractive reading of every description.

ALLEGED STABBING AFFRAY AT ORFORD.

On Sunday afternoon, a quarrel took place near Orford Hall, Warrington, between two men named Thomas Golding, 1, Halifax-street, and Joseph Brookes, 2. Gandy's-yard, off Dial-street, and a number of farm labourers, and during the course of the dispute, it was alleged that Brookes pulled his knife out of his pocket, and stabbed in the arm one of the labourers named Thomas Fahay, indicting a serious wound. Brookes and Golding were subsequently arrested at their places of abode and taken to the police station, but as the affray took place about sixty yards beyond the borough bound- ary, the case was outside the jurisdiction of the borough bench of magistrates. The two men were brought before the borough bench on Monday morning,and after formal evidence had.been given, they were handed over to the county police.

Lat.'. on in• the same morning, the prisoners were brought to the Occasional Court before Mr. James Tinsley, and were charged with unlawfully inflicting upon Thomas Fallay,on the 11th August,grievous bodily harm, with a knife. Thomas Fahay, the complainant, said that he was a labourer at Caldwell's farm, Orford. On Sunday,he was going home with two companions, Shaughnassy and Rafferty, along Sandy-lane, Orford, when they met the two prisoners. Brookes in passing knocked his elbaw against Shaughnassy, whereupon the former took off his coat and wanted to fight. He went to his coat again, and then came towards them, and Shaughnassy and Rafferty ran away. Brookes then made a charge at him (complainant) and struck at him. He put up his arm to save himself and received a severe cut with a knife on the wrist. If he had not put up his arm, he would have received the blow on the head. After the blow, Brookes and Golding went towards Warrington. He (complain- ant) lost a lot of blood, and was taken by his two com- panions to Dr. Hall's surgery,where his arm was dressed. The prisoner, Golding, never did anything to him. Both prisoners were drunk, and witness had not seen either of them before Sunday afternoon. _ . . .

John Shaughnas:l7 gave similar evidence,and said that he saw Brookes pull his knife out of his pocket and strike Fahay with it. John Rafferty also gave corroborative evidence and said that after striking Fahay, Brookes cut his hat to pieces with the knife. P.C. Chrimes said that at about 5.30 on Sunday after- noon, he recoived information of the occurrence and went to Dr. Hall's surgery, when he saw the com- plainant who had a large wound on the back of the wrist, one of the sinews being severed, and several stitches being required. As he found that the affray took place outside the borough boundary, witness saw P.C. Johnson, of the county constabulary, and late at night they went to the house of Brookes. The prisoner, who was upstairs, refused to come down and was the worse for drink. Further assistance was obtained, and the house was surrounded with constables, but prisoner got through the bedroom window on to the house top, and it was threequarters of an hour before they could I capture him and take him to the police station where Fahay identified him as being the man who had wounded him._ Golding was arrested late the same night.

P.C. Johnson said that when he received Brookes and Golding into custody, he charged them with unlawfully wounding Thomas Fahay, and Brookes replied that he could do enough with his fists if it came to a test. Gold- ing said that he had nothing to do with it. The two men were searched and a knife produced was found on Gold- ing,but nothing on Brookes. On the way to the court that morning Brookes said that Fahay had a knife, and he (Brookes) tried to take it off him'and cut his little finger in doing so. He managed to get the knife and threw it into the wheat field near by. The prisoners were remanded until next Tuesday, bail being allowed to Golding.

MR. GLADSTONE'S HANDS.

A lady chiralngist describes in the " Palmist " her impression of Mr. Gladstone's hands, gained from half an hour's close observation of them whilst his luggage was being transferred from the Tantalloon Castle to the special train at Tibury. "He was part of the time in animated conversation, so that I saw the wonderful gesture, and the immense power and individuality of the Jupiter finger. That Jupiter finger is short, but very heavy. But what seemed to me most remarkable was the very long Apollo finger, not half a nail's length shorter than the Saturn, and the Mercury, very pointed, the same length from the tip of the Apollo, so that the Mercury was really a nail's length shorter than Saturn. The nails of both hands were very bright and clear and pile in colour, and the hands very white. I could not, of course, SP(' the Lines or Mounts, excepting Luna, which was very large troin the percussion." Chirologists must find out tor themselves what all that means.

The Administrative Council of the Independent Labour party have decided to call upon the members of tire party to contest all by-elections as they occur. At a meeting of the party on Tuesday it was agreed "That out of the thirty seats fought by the party at the last election at least twenty should be provided with candidates." Mr. flay lie believes that at the next election the party " will probably secure 500,000 votes.

WORKINGMEN'S GOSPEL TEMPERANCE MISSION.

On Sunday night a Gospel Temperance meeting was held a•.; the end of Bewsey-street, conducted by Mr. John Drury. Mr. R. Storey pointed out the .need of the Christian Church freeing herself from the drink traffic. Her alliance with it was a sin against Christianity. Mr Adam Horrocks observed that some people thought because he had not been in the habit of taking drink he knew nothing of its evils, but he would remind his hearers that it was not necessary for a man to put his finger in the fire in order to know the effect. He saw much of the evil of drinking, and therefore pleaded with those present to leave off drinking and sign the pledge. Some persons at the end of the meeting acted upon this advice, and took the pledge. CYCLES.—Gintaxo,Sole Agent, Bridge-at., Warring- ton.

SERIOUS FARM FIRE NEAR. WARRINGTON. Shortly after midnight on Friday last week some farm labourers employed by Messrs. Edwards, Antrobus House farm, Antrobus, were awakened by seeing the outbuildings in flames. They at once raised an alarm and the fire engine at Arley Hall was sent for. In the meantime the fire had got such a firm hold, that the utmost difficulty was ex- perienced in rescuing the horses and cattle from the burning buildings, but all were eventually got out. The large stock of hay and straw was entirely destroyed, together with a quantity of harness,im- plements and machinery, lurries, &c. The dwelling- house was in considerable danger, but on the ar- rival of the Arley engine, the brigade, seeing it was hopeless to save the outbuildings, directed their at- tention to the house,and managed, by hard work,to prevent any turther damage. The stables, sbippons, and other outbuildings were completely destroyed, nothing but the ruined walls being left. The damage done, which is covered by insurance, will amount to a very considerable sum. _

ATTEMPTED SUICIDE.

At the Warrington Occasional Court, on Monday, before Mr. James Tinsley, Helen Quinn, domestic servant, 19 years of age, was charged with having attempted to commit suicide by jumping into the river Mersey, at Sankey, on the Bth inst. The de- fendant was seen at about nine o'clock on the morn- ing of the day named to walk along the bank to the river, and after standing near to Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co.'s Works, a few minutes, with her face buried in her shawl, to jump in the water. A man who saw this drew the attention of another man to the occurrence. Together they followed her into the water, and after much difficulty she was rescued, and placed upon the bank in an unconscious state. After cautioning the girl, whose head was said to have been affected in consequence of a certain accident, the magistrates discharged her, Mrs. Bate, her mistress, promising to take care of her.

The members of the above Brigade, who had re- maine d in the northern camp on South Shore Blackpool, during the whole of the week, returned on Saturday afternoon. On Wednesday the various companies were drilled with the Volunteers in camp on South Shore. and the smart manner in which the lads acquitted themselves deservedly called forth loud praise. They were inspected by General Carey during the week, the Parlgate Com- pany joining the Warrington St. Elphin's Company. The General expressed himself as thoroughly satis- fied with their appearance and drill. Though the weather during the week was somewhat showery, on the whole the lads thoroughly enjoyed camp life. Lieutenant-Colonel J, D. Buckton was in command of the Warrington Battalion.

TRH SAD BURNING FATALITY AT WARRINGTON. Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy coroner, held an inquest in the Parade-room of the Police Station on Mon- day afternoon, touching the death of Itlary Kelly, the one-year-old child of Elizabeth Sankey Bridges.—Elizabeth Kelly, mother of the deceased, ' said that shortly after eight o'clock on Friday morning she heard screams coming from the bedroom in which the deceased and another child had been sleeping. Going up- stairs she found the bed in flames, and the room full of smoke. She rescued the children, and after examination found the deceased was severely burnt about the head. She at once took her to the Infirmary. Questioned by the Coroner, witness re- plied that some matches had been left in a small drawer in the dressing table, but she could not ex- plain how the children had procured them.—Nurse Dobson, of the Warrington Infirmary. testified to reeeivinct the child into the institution at about 9-30 on Friday morning,when it was suffering from severe burns on the head and left arm and in conse- quence of which it died in the Infirmary at about half-past three on the afternoon of the same day.— The Jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death.'

NOTES oN GAMIiS AND

ATHLETICS.

CRICKET.

The match between Oldham and Warrington on Sat- urday was completely spoiled by the rain, and Warring- ton were thus unable to emphasise their forma- victory, or Oldham were unable to take their revenge whichever it might have been. All the Warrington team had their innings, but they were all dismissed with meagre figures with the exception of Podmore and Woods, who scored 19 and 22 respectively.

The Warrington team was a very weak one which journeyed to Oldham, for they were minus R. Smith, J.J. Crosfield, S. M. Crosfield, and Connor, and it would not therefore have been much of a surprise had the " Roughyeds " won. A total of 77 is not a very large one, and might easily have been passed. Warrington are becomming champion drawists, as they have had six games now which have not been brought to a conclusion.

To-day the W arringtonians meet Sefton, and should make a determined effort to reverse the verdict of the first meeting. The Seftonians were the first team which Warrington had to meet this season, and a miserable ex- hibition was made. It is to be hoped that the team will make a much bettor show to-day.

The averages this week have not undergone much change. The whole of the players who took part in the match last Saturday have lowered their average, with the exception of Podmore and E. Woods. H. Hind who has been at the top for about three weeks, has dropped third,as he only scored five on Saturday, while Crostield and Smith did not play, and, therefore, have the ad- vantage in this respect of Hind's failure. Bates and Clark stick close to each other yet, while Podmore and Callon have each completed their hundred runs. There are now eight who have accomplished this feat, but un- less some good score is made, it is not likely that this number will be added to within the next few weeks,the next highest being E. England with 64. Lieutenant Tupman has had very bad luck with the bat, and it will be noticed that his average is three for four innings. His twelve runs were made in one innings, so that the remaining three innings have resulted in the unpopular cypher. His luck will perhaps turn, but it will be too late soon.

The following is the table of averages up to Saturday last : times

innings not out runs average 33 Crosfield R. Smith H Hind Lieu. Plomer Bates Clark (A) W IL Podmore 9 1W Mortimer E England 8 3 Callon E Woods G Clarke W Wordsworth 6 11 Connor Lieu. Tupman.. 4

Like the senior match, the match between the second teams of Warrington and Oldham was brought to no settled termination owing to the rain. The War- rington team batted first, and some, very respectable scoring broueht the total to 143. Towards this Brown contributed 45, Hammon 38 not out, and Rushworth 21, while Mr. " Extras " obliged with 14. pad the rain kept off, a victory for Warrington was morally certain, for three of the visitors were out for 13. Warrington had however to rest content with a moral victory.

The C.L.C. Engineers defeated the Sankey Wire Mills on Saturday, and thus showed that they are in fine form. There is no doubt that they are a strong junior team and will take some beating. They have now in two succes- sive weeks vanquishes Sankey Wire Mills, and Sankey Weslevans, both on "foreign soil," and ought to be well sat;sfied with themselves. It is soma time since the Wire Mills have suffered defeat, and it was only on the previous week that they beat Irlam at Liam, a feat that had not been accomplished for two or three years. The Engineers second team were not so fortunate as the first, for the Sankey Wire Mills second proved too much for them, and equalised things a little, a result which, by the way, was obtained at the first meeting of the teams.

Orappenhall St. Wilfrid's appear to have recovered from the knocking about they received at the hands of Llandudno, and they polished off the non-commissioned officers of Orford in fine style on Saturday, for after the non-comms were dismissed for 57, the Saints put on 80 for the loss of only three wickets.

The fixt-ures in first-class cricket have again been seriously interferred with by the rain. At one time it appeared as though Surrey would again escape defeat by the aid of Jupiter Plavius, but the match came to a termination after all, very much to the satisfaction of orkshire. This is the second defeat Surrey has received this season, and as during the past ten years, the Tykes have, with the present, only beaten Surrey three times, Surrey have no right to grumble. Yorkshire have, how- ever, in the aggregate a tremendous majority of wins.

===== 0002601_18950817_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALL.

The football season will soon be here again, and it does not seem long since the close of last season. Asso- ciation football commences a fortnight on Monday, but the Rugh; people are not so greedy. and will delay until the third Saturday in Septenaber. Eight months in the year is surely long enough for anybody, but in Scotland the football season is now in full swihg,and has been for some weeks. They also continue until the end of May, so that they scarcely have time for a rest at all. gngland is much more sensible in this matter at any rate than the Scotch.

The coming season in England will be a very memor- able one. The Association code will be much as usual, and in the first division of the League, much curiosity will be experienced to see whether Bury will have as inglorious a career as Liverpool had last season. It was a little curious that Liverpool should Se the champions of the second league and go through the fixtures without a single defeat, and then be the bottom club of the first division. Bury will no doubt do their utmost not to follow this example.

In Rugby circles there will be much curiosity to see how things work under the complications which have arisen. The Yorkshire first League clubs are in a very peculiar position. They are now debarred from being in the cup ties unless they go through the preliminary stages, and they have no member on the Yorkshire Union committee, although they are subject to it. They will alb be excluded when the choice for a county team to represent Yorkshire has to be made. How the clubs will act,or whether they will keep tozether is a question which will be a very interesting one to see answered.

In Lancashire, the clubs of the first IPREUP have not gone to the length of their Yorkshire brethren, but still they have declined to take any further part in the league which is under the auspices of the Lancashire Union. Salford and Swinton have declined to go with the other nine, and by the fiat of the Union, the noble nine cannot take part in any competition. The newspap•rs of the county are, however,not under the con,rol of any Union of that kind, and there is not the shadow of a doubt but that the table will be kept as though the clubs formed a league. The action of the clubs is practically a protest against the way in which Manchester monopo- lises the management of the affairs of Lancashire Rugby football.

===== 0002601_18950817_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
THE IRON TRADE.

There was a somewhat limited attendance at the Birmingham iron market on Thursday, attributable to the holidays. Quite a business tone, however, pre- vailed, and transactions were rather above the average. Prices for all classes of iron were firm, and producers were reluctant to make the usual abatements. Forward delivery contracts could only be placed at slightly enhanced terms upon current rates. Ordinary Stafford- shire bars were fully maintained at the association minimum, and small lots could not he bought under £5 10s. delivered in the district. It is expected that at the meeting of the Unmarked Bar Association, which has been postponed till next week, the list will be advanced to £5 103. at works. Makers of specialties such as channel, tee, guide, and saeh iron, received a fair number of orders, and the leading makers commanded their own terms. Sash-iron was quoted £7 ss. to £9. according to quality, and channel iron Di to £O3 10s. Chain and cable bars were in better request in anticipation of the new orders to be distributed by the British Admiralty. Marked bars moved off rather more freely at steady rates, and sheets were in brisk request, at £6 17s. 6d. to £7 for doubles and £7 101. to £7 12s. (id. for fattens, an advance of half-a-crown a ton on latter quotations. The galvanizers continue busy no home and export arders, and a further advance in the association minimum is expected. Pig-iron was well supported, the demand having overtaken the supply ; hut , makers are not disposed to increase the output until' prices are more remunerative. As a rule makers have a good reserve of orders, some of them extending until the end of the year. Northamptonshire forge pig was quoted 394. to 40s. and Derbyshire is. extra, Staffordshire all- mine pig realizing 55.. to 603. according to quality, and Welsh best forge hematite 555. delivered in Staffordshire.

THE SHOCKING FATALITY ON THE SHIP CANAL.

THE INQUEST. Mr. J. E. Woreley held the inquest on Friday after- noon at the Pickering Arms, Thelwall, touching the - death of the two men killed last week on the Ship Canal , at Thelwall. Superintendent Britton was present on • behalf of the police, and Mr. Arthur Newton, solicitor, • was present on behalf of the Canal Company. I John George Whitlow, Thelwall, farmer, said that I deceased, Edward Whitlow, was his brother and was 29 years of age. He was a labourer on the Canal. Witness was not present at the time ef the accident but saw him the night before, when he was in his usual health. Isabella Lambeth, wife of George Lambeth, 223, Mersey-view, LatclafOictsaid that John Parnell lodged at her house about six months ago. He was 53 years of age. He was a labourer on the Canal, but she had not seen him for about six months. She identified the body. Alfred Robbins, Thelwall, assistant engineer, said that a lock gate which had been injured by an accident some months had been floated from Latchford Locks, ago, and was in course of being drawn out of the water on to the land at Thelwall on Thursday, it being taken to the shed at Thelwall for repair. A slip way had been constructed and the gate was placed upon it under water. The gate was being hoisted out of the Canal by means of four sets of blocks and tackle, also by two derricks and four winches. The tackle was attached to the lock gate by means of four slings constructed of steel wire, with eyes on either end attached to the lower blocks. The wire of the slings was 4 3-8 inches circum- ference. About 3.30 p.m. the men were re-arranging . each separate set of tackle one at a time. They had changed number one set to a point which was necessary, round the gate making number one fast. They then proceeded to change number four leaving number one, two, and three fast. The gate was at the time almost out of the water, up the slip. The change was foe the purpose of getting a grip lower down. About half the gate was resting on land, the top portion on the balance , being raised up in the air. Number three sling suddenly broke without the slightest warning. It was of the same material as the other slings, but was a new one. When the sling broke it caused one end of the gateto slip towards the canal taking up the slack rope of number four which was still fastened on to the gate,but was slackened off the winch to allow it to be moved farther down. Two men (the deceased) who were bawling in the" slack " were tossed into the air when the rope tightened, and were killed instantly. He could not account for the rope breaking. Number two sling broke by the " Mewing " ef the gate. Witness was en the gate at the time of the accident every care was taken at the removal of the gate. One gate had been ha wled up the slip in the same manner that the gate in question was being hawled,with the same slings except the one that broke. But this sling was made from the same cuil of rope, and should therefore have stood the strain better than the others. By a Juryman : Other men about five or six were em- ployed near by. There was not as much strain upon the rope as there had been previously, as the gate was stationary and there was no friction to overcome. William Cooke, 26 John-street, labourer on the Canal, said that he was standing behind number 2 winch. He saw Whitlow thrown into the air and Parnell fall over by the tightening of the rope. He went to the two men, Whitlow was cut open near the heart and was quite dead. Parnell was injured in the head and neck. Witness thought that the man's neck was knocked out. —By a juryman : Everything was quiet at the time the accident occurred. The deceased were standing by the rope pulling it at the time of the accident, and seven or eight more men. Jacob Whitehouse, 13, Thelwall-lane, waterman, said that he was present at the time of the accident. He gave details similar to the witness Robbins. The rope at the time of the accident threw witness to the ground. Both men were dead when witness got to them. William Fox, mechanical engineer for the Ship Canal under Mr. Hunter, said that he was superintending the work in connection with the hoisting of the gate. He was present when the accident happened. The slings were made of best steel. They were made by the War- rington Wire Rope CompanyMhe wire had been tested, •he ascertained,and stood a test of 45 tons. The gate was about 120 to 128 tons,complete, but several portions were taken off. At the time of the accident he did not think half the weight of the gate was upon the alines. The other gate had been got up precisely the same way as that one,and the other gate hung up in the same position for about twelve hours. The weight on number three sling was in his opinion not equal to the brealring strain by several times. He could not suggest any better method of moving the gate,and it would still have to be moved in the same manner. He was unable to say how the rope broke. A portion of the rope was then brought in for inspection. Robert Chadwick, Bewsey-road, works manager for the Warrington Wire Rope Company, said that the Corn- panv were makers of the rope from which the slings were made. The break in the case was a clean break, but one or two wires were " chafed." The rope was 45 tons single, but being double it was about 80 tons strain. The rope was a good rope, and the wires composing it were tested. William Henry Hunter,M.l.C.E., engineer of the Man- chester Ship Canal Company, said that he was present when the accident occurred, Mr. Fox being in charge of the work under his supervision. He was on the gate a minute before the accident, and he could almost feel that the gate was on the balance. He thought the breakage was rather short, and that the steel was rather hard. Steel was a critical material and its composition affected its strength. He could actually arrive at no conclusion as to why the rope broke. The weight of the gate completely equipped was 130 tons. At the time of the accident it was partly dismantled and he estimated the weight to be 125 tons at the out- side. More than half the weight was taken off, and he did not think there was more than 50 tons weight upon the slings. Any one sling ought to have been able to carry the weight. There was also no jerk to cause the accident. Witness saw no sign of "punishment "upon the rope whatever, and the rope did not break at the point where it was liable to be strained, but at some distance from the gate.He did not think that any further inquiry would elucidate the matter. The only possible solu- tion was that there was some difference in the quality of the steel. The Company always got the very best steel, and in his opinion, they had allowed adequate margin for the strain which was to be put upon the ropes on the present occasion. Mr. Newton said that the Company had taken every precaution they possibly could, and the accident was one , which was unforeseen completely. The coroner said that they had heard the story of the accident from both workmen and officials, and they had also had a witness from the Wire Ropery. It had been , brought out that the arrangements for the getting out of the gates had been made in the most approved manner. The jury had had the best assistance they possibly could have as they had the evidence of Mr. Hunter whom, he was sure, they would all believe when he said that he I was as anxious as any of them to get te the bottom of that accident. He had given them all the information in his power which his great experience would enable I him to give. The only explanation which Mr. Hunter appeared able to give was that the steel rope might have I been harder in that particular part and therefore could not bear as much tension as the other part. There was, however, no accounting for it by any jerk, as Mr. Hunter was on the gate a minute before the accident occurred, he would have seen it had there been any. He (Mr. Worsley) did not think that any expert would be abla to give any further in- formation upon the matter, and he therefore thought that the verdict ought to be " Accidental death "from the breaking of the rope, but that there was not suffici- ent evidence to show the cause of the breaking of the rope. He thought that the Company had done all possible to prevent ony accident. The jury returned a verdict in accordance with the coroner's instructions. and Mr. Hunter informed the coroner that the Company would pay the funeral ex penses of the two deceased men.

The gate which caused such a shocking accident cn the Ship Canal at Thelwall on Thursday evening was successfully pulled out of the Canal on Sunday after- noon. Since the accident the men had been engaged in repairing the tackle, and os Siturday evening every- thing was again ready for the final effort of drawing the gate completely on land. Rain put a stop to their work for that day, and it was found necessary to resume operations on Sunday mrning. The work was accom- plished shortly after two o'clock on Sunday, and the gate was safely secured. During the morning the scene was visited by many persons, who were curious to see the spot where the accident occurred. The three injured men—Michael Scully, John Gibson, and Peter Leigh— are fast recovering from the ill effects of the accident, and are expected to be ablest no very distant date to resume their work.—The body of George Parnell was interred at about four o'clock on Sunday afternoon in the burial ground adjoining the Church of All Saints, Thelwall. It has since transpired that Parnell was a widower,having four children—two sons and two daugh- ters—and at the time of his death was 58 years of age. The funeral ceremony was conducted by the Rev. G. W. Grundy, of the South American Missionary Society,there being many of the deceased man's fellow workers pre- sent, some of whom bore the coffin to the grave. On Monday afternoon the body of Edward Whitlow was in- terred in the Thelwall churchtard. Whitlow was only 29 years of age, and leaves two young children and a widow. The latter has been greatly affected by her husband's premature death, and is in a delicate state of health at present. The funeral ceremony was witnessed by a large number of relatives and friends of the de- ceased, the coffin being carried by several of the deceased's fellow-workmen. The service was conducted by th 3 Rev. T. Pym Williamson.

A MILLIONAIRE'S WILL.

Probate of the will has been granted and estate duty- has been paid oa £1.821.281 3. Bd. as the net value, the gross value being £1,824,483 Bs. 10d., of the personal estste of Mr. George Henry Strut; of Bridgehill, Belper, Derbyshire. who died on the 14th April last, aged sixty_. nine years,the eldest son of the late Mr. Zedekiah Strutt. Mr. Strutt's will bears date October 18th, 1894, and the sole executor thereof is his son, Mr. George Herbert Strutt,of Ma kering House, Derby, whose co-trustees are Mr. John Borough and Mr. John Hunter, to each of Whom the testator bequesths £5OO ; to his daughter Mrs. Lucy Frances Irwin, wife of Major Thomas Angelo Irwin, £lB,OOO ; to his grandson Georee Ashton Strutt, when twenty-one years of age, £20,000 ; to each other grandson on his attaining the age of twenty-one years,. £15,000; and t) each grand-daughter £lO,OOO. Mr. Strutt devises to his daughter Mrs. Irw n abso- lutely the Field Head House property, and he bequeaths upon trusts £140,000, to pay 'he 13come thereof to Mrs. Irwin during her life, and subject to her life interest, to hold the trusts fund in trust f r her children, or in default of issue as she may app in. Tes- tator bequeaths to his wife, Mrs. Agnes Ann Strutt, daughter of Mr. E. Ashton, of Prescot, Lancashire, £3,000 his furniture and household effects, horses and carriages, his volumes of Turner's sketches, volumes of Corboulks sketches, a picture of Christ at Emmaus, by Cope, and a life annuity of £7,003. Mrs. Strutt is to have the use and enjoyment of Bridgehill during her life and the advowson of Bridgehill is left to her al solutely. All the residue of his proprrty, real and personal, alr. Strutt devised and bequeathed to his son Mr. George Herbert Strutt absolutely, but in the event of his death in the testator's lifetime the estate was to be in trust for his son who should first attain the age of 21 years, but subject to the setting apart of portions of £50,000 for each other child of Mr. George Herbert Strutt. Each person entitled in that event to the settled estate was to assume, ;f not already bearing, the name and arms of Strutt. The trustees are authorised to invest upon the security of landed estate in England, but not elsewhere, and upon certain bonds and debentures, but not upon these of mining, banking, insurance, or finance com- panies.
