_ SHIP CANAL.

PREFERENTIAL RATES ON THE CANAL. In the Court of the Railway and Canal Commission.. on Saturday, the case of the Ship Canal v. The Shrop- shire Union Railway, &c., Company, was again before , the court on a summons by the Ship Canal asking for an order of the court that paragraph three in answer to the Shropshire Company should be struck out, on the ground of its being embarassing and irrelevant to any issue in the action. Mr. Baifour Browne, on behalf of the Canal Company, said his lordship was aware of the ' facts which related to the through rates from Eastham to certain mills in Staffordshire. Paragraph three was in reference to the route, and was to the effect that if a through rate was granted from Ellesmere Port to the Cobden Flour Mills. Company at Wrexham ought not to be by the route proposed, but by the route hitherto adopted for similar traffic to the same destination from the quays at Ellesmere Port to the station, and by the lines of the Shropshire Union to Chester, and by the London and North-Western and Great Western joint lines to its destination. Counsel said the objection of the Ship Canal Company was that it was the first time the railway had ever mentioned a route. A through rate could be proposed by any forwarding company, and in the meantime, the Canal Company said they were a for- warding company. Mr. Bal'our Browne proceeded to contend that the railway company had objected without giving the grounds of the objection, The Ship Canal Company treated the only serious objection to be the. legal one which they came into court on the previous day to argue. Mr. C. A. Russell, on behalf of the Shropshire Union Railway Company, contended that the paragraph could not be said to be irrelevant, as it dealt with matters which the court must give an opinion upon. Mr. Balfour Browne having replied on the whole case,

Mr. Justice Collins, in giving judgment, said this ap- plication raised a point of some difficulty. He went a lone way with the arguments of Mr. Balfour Browne that the Act contemplated, on an application by persins interested for a through rate, those persons being for- warding companies, that they should say whether they agreed to do so or not. If they did not agree, they should give notice of their objection, and the grounds of their objection. But Mr. Russell's points were squally of importance, and, though thizaking it was to be depre- cated that the paragraph was put in the answers in this way, it should not be struck out. The court ruled that the application should be dismissed without costs. THE STANLEY FORCE COLLISION.—JUDGMENT. In the Admiralty Court,London,mi Fridav,Mr. Justice Barnes, who was assisted by Trinity Masters, delivered his judgment in the action for damages brought by the owners of the Liverpool screw-steamer Stanley Force against the Manchester Ship Canal Company, whose twin-screw hopper Beta came into collision with the plaintiff's steamship on July 5, in the Eastham channel of the Mersey, with the result that the Stanley Force sank almost immediately,and her captain and four of her crew lost their lives. The Stanley Force was on a voy- age from Weston Point to PetPrkeed with a cargo of salt and the Beta was returning to the canal after discharg- ing her dredglngs at sea.—Sir Walter Phillimore and Mr. Butler Aspinall appeared for the plaintiffs,and Mr. Pyke, Q.C., and Mr. Hope for the defendants. In the course, of his judgment his Lordship said the points made by the defenoants against the Stanley Force had not been es- tablished. The Stanley Force kept to her proper side of the channel, and although placed at a disadvantage in not having her master and mate, who were on the bridge at the time of the collision, to give evidence, the pilot had given his evidence fairly and honestly, and the con- clusion which he (the judge) had come to was that he acted properly in every respect. The Beta was solely in fault for not passing the Stanley Force portto port, for not stopping and reversing sooner than she did. On her own evidence she only stopped and reversed at the last moment. On these ernunda the Beta must be held alone. to blame. Judgment accordinely for the plaintiffs with costs, the damages to be assessed in the Registry in the usual way.

There are re sons for fearing that the outbreaks of which formation has already been received illaY be but; the opening incidents of a widespread and fuer"n or less organised campaign against the Europe missionaries and Christian converts. It has been predicted by those who are well acquainted with the feelings and the methods of the Chinese thq the foreigners in China would, after the results of .the war with Jap an known, be troubled zw ithbecaannie kf goeulithertaelal of native fanaticism. 11 T -ea" predictions have been verified in a terrible fashion. The Chinese are smarting under the sting of a hurniliat- ing defeat at the hands of a foe they affeetecif hold in contempt, and in their blind and criminating rage they lump together all who ;ars- not natives of their own country as devils" and enemies. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

FOUND IN a. LOFT la lII7NCORN.

_ At Runcorn Police Court on Thureday morn*: before Mr. H. Taylor, John Hedgeoock,of Runcora, Was charged with sleeping out the previous night. P.O. Sutler, who stated the case, deposed that be found the prisoner in a bit over some shippons near Savagea' 'Bridge. Prisoner was discharged with a caution. PAPER ON " GAMBLINO."

At the fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Sons of Temperance on Saturday eveoing, Mr. George W. Holt read a paper on " Gamblinz." The chair was occupied by Mr. Thomas Heesom and there was a good atten- dance. RUNCORN COUNTY COURT.

There was little business to transact at the sitting oE this court on Tuesday before His Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, The cases comprised 15 plaints and 25 judg- ment summonses. There were no disputed cases of public interest. A RUNCORNIAN IN TROUBLE AT FRODSHAM. At Frodsham Police Court on Monday morning before Mr. Thotams, a charge of drunkennesss was preferred against Elizabeth White, of Brunswick-street, Runcorn. The evidence adduced showed that on Saturday night the prisoner was found in a helpless state of drunkenness in Ship-street, Frodsham by P.C. Wetherley. A fine of 53. including costs' or in default seven days imprisonment was imposed. She went to prison. LOCAL PROCTORS OF CONVOCATION. The Rev. Canon Maitland Wood,vicar of Runcorn,and the Rev. William. E. Torr, vicar of St. Mary's, Easthas; have been elected Proctors of Convocation for the Province of York for the Archdeac,inry of Chester, Mr. Torr taking the place held since 1874 by the Rev. Canon Dodd, vicar ef Stretton, near Warrington. RUNCORN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY'S EXCURSION. As will be seen from an advertisement in another column, the annual excursion in connection with the Runcorn Co-operative Industrial and Provident Society, Limited will be held on Mon lay next. The place ot destination will be Blackpool, and the fare is Axed at 23. 9d. Particulars as to timei of st trting from Run- corn, Dittoo, Widnes, Appleton, and Farnworth will be found in the advertisement.

THE TREAT TO RUNCORN POOR CHILDREN.. The preparations for this treat which is to take place on Wednesday afternoon, when the poor children of Runcorn whose ages rtnge from 7 to 13 years, will be taken to Overton Hill and there provided with tea, are now nearly complete. At a meeting of the Scrutiny Committee on Tuesday evening at the Town Hall, the names of over 500 children were submittedand approved of, as being entitled to the treat. The offer made by the League of the Cross brass band to accompany the children was gladly accepted..

SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARY. The Rev. R. Renton, of Frodsharn, was the preacher at the anniversary Aermons in connection with the Nelson-street Wesleyan Sunday School, Runcorn, on Sunday. There were very good congregations wspecislly so in the evening. In the afternoon a service for parents and children was held, and was conducted by Mrs. Renton.Miss Clara Taylor exceedingly well sang the solo " 0 Rest in the Lord." Collections which were made in aid of the school fond z amountel to £lB. THE SHIPMENTS 07 SILT FROM RUNCORN AND WESTON

POINT.

During the month of July the shipments of salt from Runcorn and the Ship Canal ware : Rock salt 75 tons, white salt 7210 ; and from Weston Point, rock salt 150 tons, white salt 14.346 tons. Of the white salt, 352 tons were forwarded to ticitish North America, 2soo tons to the East Indies, 555 tons Denmark and Irelan 1.; 1,436 tons to England ; 1,305 to Ireland, and 15.708 ta Scot- land. The shipments show an increase of 701 tons from Runcorn and .793 tons from Westnn Point as compared with the corresponding period in 1894. PoratTIVE kiRTHODIST CHBVEL CHOIR. On Sunday the annual choir sermons in comuction with the Primitive Method,st Chapel, Greenwas-road, were held. The preacher for the occasion was Mr. J. Peters, of 13uckley, who preached morning and evening to large congregations. At both services special music was rendered. The annual treat for the scholars attending Camden Wesleyan Sunday School was held on Wednesday after- noon when about 250 journeyed he train to 'Frodsham, and afterwards proceeded to Overton Hill. Here they romped about until tea time. They then went to the tea rooms of Mrs. Parker-Hcoie at Mersey View, where an excellent tea was provided. - Sr. JOUNS PRNSBYTERIAN SUNDAY SCHOOL.

About 150 scholars at ached to this Sunday school had their annual outing to Overton on Wednesday afternoon. The weather being beautifully fine, a most enjoyable time was spent in romping about the hills. Tea was provided at the Mersey View, alter which the chi'dren walked to the station, and reached Runcorn about seven o'clock.

A NEW T'l3G.

A steel screw tug hag heen built by Messrs. Joseph T. Eltringhana and Co of South Shields, to the order of the Manchester Ship Canal Company (Bridgewater under- taking) for towage and other services. 'The tug is to trade to Runcorn. and will be under the command of Captain Jones. The dimensions of the vessel are 90ft. byl9ft. bflOft., with twin compound surface condens- ing engines, each 14 inch and 30 inch cylinders by 21 inch stroke,built by Mr. George T. Grey, South Shields, and having a large steel multitubular bollet, by Messrs. Eltringham, working at 'l2olb. pressure. In addition to the-ordinary tug arrangements, a large saloon has been fitted at the fore part of the vessel for the use of the di- rectors and the officials of the Canal Cottspanv. On the trial the machinery attained a mean speed orioi knots, and both vessel and machinery gave the greatest satis- faction to the builders, and the reoresentative of the Manchester Ship,Canal Company, Mr. Rawlinson. of Runcorn, who 'has -superintended the construction of hull and machinery. The vessel is named " Charles Galloway."

THE INSPECTION.

- - - I On Saturday, the 2nd Earl of Chester'a Volunteer Regiment concluded their week's encampment at Black- pool. The Runcorn Volunteers, who formed a portionof the regiment, arrived home about2.3o, and looked all the better tor their military experiences of the of the week. With the exception of Saturday night, the weather on the whole was satisfactory and the health of the men has been excellent. On Friday the annual inspectiun took place. The regiment was drawn up in line and receivAl the inspecting officer, Golonel Butlin, with the general salute. Both ranks having been minutely inspected, the march past was done in smart style. The men then performed the manual and firing exercise under Major Hazlehurst ; and afterwards Major Woolley, Captains Smith, Harrison, and (lawn drilled half of the battal- ion. Lieutenants Clarke, Brundrit, Thorneley, Royle, Nicholson and Garrett next-- drilled the companies, with the view to qualifying; and theattack formation brought a stiff morning's work to a close. The whole of the movements were executed with a considerable amount of military precision, and the men thoroughly deserved the praise bestowed upon them. At the conclusion of the inspection the battalion was drawn up, and several non- commissioned officers, who were about to receive the Volunteer long service medal, stood on one side.—The Inspecting Officer said he was very pleased to seethe battalion turn out to-day as it always used to do, in a very, creditable manner. The men were all clean and smart-looking, with very few exceptions. e liked to see the belts put on a little more accurately,Mid he hoped another year that might be done,but generally they were very well turned out. The march past was steadily per- formed, and the manual firing exercise was very well done, particularly the firing exercise. Li the drill tne men were very steady, and seemed to know their work well. The battle formation there was very little time to practice, but it was done very fairly on the whole. fie had a great pleasure and that was to preseat the long service medals to those who had served twenty years or upwards in the regiment. Be thought it VIM very just and good reward that those who had devoted very much of their time to the service of their country should re- ceive a token of gratitude and a mark of respect, from their county. Colonel Butlin then presented the medals 1 the band of the Regiment playing" Aull Lang Syne." 1, The following ,were the recipients of the medals ' Lance-Corporal Blane, Corporal Rigby, Sergeant Carr, Corporal Roscoe, Hon. Qeartermaster Charles Brown, Colour-Sergeants Carr, A. Davies,and T. Davies, Private Roberts, and Corporal Simcock. _

The conduct ef the Runcorn men have been excep- tionally good, not one of them having to appear at the Court Martial. They ware also successful in carrying off almost all the prizes offered during the eticampinent.The prize for the best line of tents was taken by the G Com- pany (Runcorn) ; for the s:nartest non-eomtnissioned officer, Ist prize G Company (Runcorn), 2oci prize, F Company (Runcern) ; for . the smartest privatl, lat prize, F Company (Runcorn), 2nd prize, 0 Company (Runcorn). In the competition for the best kept indivi- dual tent two prizes were offered but owlriKttp the neat- eess displayed it was decided to give three, and two of the prizes were awarded to F Company and one to G Company, both of Runcorn. The only other toriz, offered to the battalion was for the best bugler and this was won by a member of one of the Chester companies. Colenel Ashton and other officers expressed themselves as hav- ing, this year, spent the pleasantest camp in their ex- perience.

WESTON.

PARISH Cousten Merrnso.—Ptie Rev. Canon Wood presided at the meeting of the Parish Council on Friday evening.—There were also present: Messrs, D. Basnett, S. Savage, Moores, H. E. Guee, and Collier (clerk).— With regard to the emptying of ashpits, in reply to a question by the Chairman as to what was tieing done, the Clerk said he knew that Mr. Farrington,.the nuisance inspector for the Rural Council, had been round the dis- trict twice since their last meeting, but he had nothing to report to the Council.—The Chairman thought it was satisfactory to know that the matter was receiving ettention.—The Clerk reported that in accordance with instructions, he wrote to the Water Committee of the Runcorn Council complaining of the insufficient supptr, and asking for their attention to the same. Mr. Marshall afterwards replied, stating that he had laid the letter before his committee, and they would investigate the cases, and do all they could to put right any defects they might discover. He (Mr. Collier) thought that since the letter had been sent, the Runcorn Water Committee had done all they could to give Weston a better supply, and from information he had received, the supply was much better.—Mr. Basnett and Me. Guest both stated that the supply was much improved.--The Chairman thought the Council should keep their eye on the matter and suggested the appointment of a sub-committee to over-look it, and report to the next meeting.-03 the motion of Mr. Guest, seconded by Mr. Moore., a sub- committee consisting of Messrs. Basnett, Sill, and Guest was appointed.—The Clerk stated that he wrcte to Mr. Marshall, secretary to the Runcorn Council, asking for further particulars with regard to the proposed new road, but he had lint yet received a reply.—Toe Chair- man thought they should at least have received an acknowledgment of the receiptof the letter.—Regarding the dangerous pat') leading from Weston to Clifton, the Clerk stated that he had seen Mr. Griffiths with reference to it,and he had promised to mention the matter to the agent of the estate (Mr. Evans).—lt was decided that a letter be written to both the occupier and the owner calling attention to the dangerous condition of the path and asking for it to be remedied.—The Chairman asked whethe: anything further had been done with a view to the taking over of the reads at Weston Point. Th- Clerk stated that he had seen Mr. Ashton (clerk of the Rural Council), and he toldiiim that the matter was re- ceiving attention. The Local Government Board had written, making some enquiries with regard to the sewer, before granting the neceasary powers for Mc: tat ing over of the roads.—Mr. Moores asked the clerk whether he could give them any information regarding the new sewer trom Sandy-lane to the roam sewer, Weston Point—The Clerk stated that he had heard nothing at all about it.—This concluded the business.

A CAREER OF IMPOSTURE.

A clever rascal named Pierre Delanov carrip.up for trial at the Seine Assizes on TUf sday. He Was emp'nyed se a house porter in one of the large Paris hospitals from 1877 to 1881, when he seems to have come to the conchisein that the life of an in-patient Was far preferatis to the one he was living, or was ever likely to live as an Ordinary working man. ffe, therefore, resolved to pass the rest of his days inside different hospitals in the capacity of invalid. fits started by feigning ataxy, and for six years deceived the highest medical experts in Paris,so cuticusly did be appear to suffer. Re stayed in turn in all the hospitals in the city, being treated with every care and consideration, until at last he met with a doctor who persisted in applying disagreeable remedies. Delaney accordingly came to the conclusion that the time to be cured had arrived, and cured he consequently became, and was discharged. He next appeared at Lourdes, supported by crutches, and presenting every 'symptom of being hopelessly crippled. With thousands of other infirm and decrepit people he was dipped in the holy well. So efficacious did this treatment prove that ha came out another man, threw his crutches to the ground and walked, as an onlooker expressed it, "like a rural postman." The Church placed him in charge of a home for invalids. But this was too much like hard work of Dvlimov, and he soon decamped with all the motes' ho could lay his hands on. Returning to 133 tis he Was admitted te the flospital of St. Anne as suffering &oaf mental debility, hut this 1,4 not prevent him from run- ninc off one nighttliewtdiot.htohl. psfl 0 Oat sr sat hkbi inne lgdf i noir rteaosstaedadihslpiuntennasltaeigret: Tivialev.polnicee twh eree n LI; but he had become by this time too well known, and was Indicted in due Course. At the, trial he energeticalte denied that he had ever shammed, but the Curt would not believe him, and sentenced him to four years' impri- sonment with hard labour.

HALTON.

• WYSLEYAN CHURCEL-111 December last, when the pastor nf MN church, theßev.W.Lile Stnne.was rendered local)•, hie of performing his duties by ill-health. the Rev. J. 13 The wag engaged to act as supply. His labours have now been brought to an end by the appointment ef the Rev C. de Putron Glidden as Mr. Stone's successor. During the time Mr. Tite has been in Halton, he has, by big energy, his zeal, and his genial disposition, won the esteem and affection of whose amocest whom he has worked. Big presence was especially welcome in the sickroom. On Sunday evening a large congregation assembled to hear him preach his farewell Pennon, the choir rendering special music. In bidding farewell to ilia flock, be said that he had tried to enter into their life, to niake their joys his joys, and their sotrows kis sorrows. He thanked them for all their kindness to him. It was only when going away, he said, that one realized how much he had become attached to the people And place cohere he bad been called to work. He should always have very happy memories of the short time he had spent at Halton.

THE L. AND N,W, RAILWAY COMPANY AND THE SHIP CANAL

Lord Ste'bridge, nresidiwr over the half•vearlv meeting of the London and North-Western Railway Company on Tuesday, spoke of the competition In the Scotch traffic He denied that. the Company had engaged in " racing' to Aberdeen, They had simply gone on in their own way, determined not to lose traffic. They had main-, rained their own, and were detelmined to maintain it. At the same time they were perfectly wflhin to meet the other companies concerned, hut the regult of any such rii.oussion or consideration would he in the end that the London and North-Western would not be the last in the race, whatever happened. Minding to the onnstruction of the branch line to the Manchester Ship Gina! from Weaste, the Chairman remarked it would dhow to the Shin Canal authorities that they wr:re not opposed to their undertaking, but that they Wished to work in with them as well as they, possibly could to the mutual advantage of both. A dividend at the rate of 5* percent. was declared.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

===== 0002601_18950817_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
TRUESDAYTHE QUEEN'S SPEECE.

The real business of the first session of the new Parlia- ment was begun on Thursday. The QUEEN'S Speech was delivered by Commission in the House of Lords. It was very brief, but it contained the following references to the Chinese outrages and the Armenian massacres :—" I deeply regret to say that most atrocious outrages upon a body of English missionaries have been reported from the province of Fukien in China. In reply to earnest representations addressed to the Chinese Government by my directions, active measures, which I trust will prove effective, are being taken for the punishment of the murderers and of all persons who are in any degree responsible for these crimes. Internal troubles have broken out in the Armenian districts of Asiatic Turkey, and have been attended with horrors which have moved k'm indignation of the Christian nations of Europe gener- ally, and of my people especially. My Ambassador and the Ambassadors of the Emperor of Russia and of the President of the French Republic, acting together, have suggested to the Government of the Sultan the reforms which, in their opinion, are necessary to prevent a recurrence of constant disorder. These proposals are now being considered by His Imperial Majesty the Sultan, and I am anxiously awaiting his decision. On the subject of legislation it said :—" At this season I of the year it will probably be found more convenient to defer .to another session the consideration of any im- portant legislative measures except those which are necessary to provide for the administrative charges of the year." The Duke of Matunonorott moved and Lord AmmmiLL seconded the ad- dress of the Lords in reply to the speech. The Earl of ROSEBERY, in the course of his remark, said as to the causes of the Liberal defeat, he was not sure that the liquor qaestion had not more to do with it than either Home Rule or the House of Lords. He was still of opinion that our best hope of seeing Irish busi- ness satisfactorily carried on was by means of the Irish themselves, but he admitted that we had got to bring more conviction to the heart and mind of what he form- ally called "the predominant partner." Laoki,w at the men chosen for the new Catinet he ventured a hope that the new Government was not Indifferent to the problem of Ireland.— Lord SALISBURY in replying,said with regard to Armenia that the Government had accepted the policy that their predecessors initiated. They had not yet succeeded in obtaining adequate guarantees for the execution of the Promised reforms by the Porte, and it was to that ob- ject that their efforts would be directed. Lord SALIS- BURY added : If generation after generation, cries of misery come up from various parts of the Turkish Em- pire, I RBI sure the SULTAN cannot blind himself to the probability that Europe will some time or other become weary of the appeals that are made to it, and the facti- tious strength that is given to his Empire will fail it. I have earnestly tried to impress upon the Turkish Government the extreme gravity of the line of conduct which it has pursued."

In the House of Commons, after the return of the Speaker and other members from the Heuse of Lords, where they had heard the QUEEN'S Speech read, the Arromssy GENERAL moved that a copy of the conviction of Mr. JOHN DALY (now in Portland prison), who was returned at the general election for Limerick, be laid before the House, along with other papers relating there- to. Mr. J. REDMOND protested against the decision of the electors of Limerick being overridden, but the motion was carried by 314 to 77. The ATTORNEY GENERAL moved that the documents be taken into con- sideration on Monday, and this was agreed to. The Address to the Throne in reply tc the QUEEN'S Speech was moved by Mr. T. W. LEGH. who admitted at thp outset that Her Majesty's Speech "had very little in it." He said he believed that the moral of the general election was that people were less anxious for legislative changes than they were supposed to be, and that what they were above all anxious for was good, sound, practical administration.— Mr. T. H. ROBERTSON seconded the Address Sir W. HARCOURT said he hoped there :would be no fal- tering on the part of the Government of this country in insisting that reforms in Armenia should be effvtually carried out. Mr. BALFOUR replying to the request of Sir W. FlAncouar for information as to legislation, the right hon. gentleman said that land legislation for Ire- land must undoubtedly be brought forward next session, and when next the QUEEN'S Speech was read it would be their duty to lay before the House a substantive pro- gramme of legislation. Mr. J. REDMOND moved an amendment to the Address cAling for a Government de- claration of nolley on the Home Rule and Irish land question. Mr. DILLON Slid the feeling in Ireland in these matters remained unchanged. Mr. G. BALFOUR said the attitude of the Government on the question of Home Rule was one of unchanging and inflexible opposition. The Land Bill introduced in the spring of next year would he in sufficient time to prevent any tenant suffer- ing any possible hardship. The debate was adjourned at midnight. A few minutes previously Dr. TANNER was suspended for disorderly conduct.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BT OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

The Runcorn Volunteers who form part of the 2n4 Bari of Chester's Regiment are to be congratu- lated upon the way they conducted themselves during the recent encampment at Blackpool. Not only did they secure an excellent character for good behaviour, but they also succeeded in carry off all the prizes offered for competition to the battalion. with one single exception. This should surely he highly satisfactory to all concerend. Another pleasing feature of the encampment was the" very good report of the Inspector, which went to prove that the men are well acquainted with their duties. Judging by the appearance of the Volunteers on their return home on Saturday, I one was led to believe that the week's camping out had been beneficial to them, and it is questionable whether a great number of them would have had the-benefit of a week at the seseide, had it not been for their connection with the Volunteers.

Well done Runcorn Cycle Club I To raise £4O for two Pu eh deserving institutions as the Run- cern District. Nursing Fund and the National Lifeboat Institution is no mean accomplish- ment and well deserves recognition. Every- thing tended to the success of the Lantern Parade, which, as everyone knows, was the means of raising the sum mentioned. The weather was ideal, and the ptpceodings were carried through without the slightest hitch. The object of all con- nected with it wee the success of the exhibition, and tb. t they were sircssful, was generally admitted. The display was far superior to its predecessor, last year, but on that occasion the elements were against. it. The collections too, Are nearly double, and with such a gratifying result, it is to be hoped that the Cycle Club will be encour- aged to make it an annual affair.

Very shortly. end that within the next few weeks, the new Technical Institute at Runcorn is to. be formally opened. The work of fitting no and furnishing the various rooms is now completed.and it may be here mentioned that Mr. Welding who has superintended the erection of the building has introdueed runny imnrovements of his own, which redound greatly to his credit. No money has been spared to make the institute n model one, and what is now wanted is the active co-operation of the inhabitants of Runcorn in making it a busy and useful home ot technical instruction.

PICNICS FROM RUNCORN.

• Mr. Patker's adult Bibli Class connected with Cam- den Wesleyan Sunday school, numbering about 30, bad an outing to Helsby on Saturday. They proceeded in waggonettes and greatly appreciated the drive. After rambling about the hills they partook of tea. The home- ward journey was shortly afterwards commenced, and was safely completed.—On Monday the members of Camden Wesles an ch:iir proceeded to LI ndudno, in company with the choirmaster, Mr. Tittle. Dinner and tea were served at one of the hotels. In the afternoon they drove round the Great Orme's Head. Fine weather prevailed and the outing w4s thoroughly enjoyed. A picnic in connection with Mrs. Morris's sewing class at Camden Wesleyan school took place on Tuesday I afternoon when about 40 members journeyed in a wag- gonette to Chester. Alter visiting places of interest in that ancient city they departed for Eccleston Ferry. After tea, provided by Mrs. Morris, a visit was paid to the Duke of Westininster's Park. The party afterwards returned to Chester where the waggonette was waiting them tor the return home. Theßtincorn Parish Church choir had their annual trip on Saturday when they proceeded to Rhyl. The torenoon was spent in various ways at Rhyl, and in the afternoon a drive was taken to St. Asaph Cathedral. Satisfac'ory arrangements were made tor the trip,which was a most enjoyahle one, by Mr. Clare.—On Monday afternoon there were two Sunday school picnics from Runcorn to Fredsham. Those connected with the Elles- mere-sr. United Methodist Free Church Sunday school, numbering over 200, had their annual trip on Monday afternoon. WIDNES MEMBERS AND SEPARATION.

On Thursday evening a meeting of Widnes members , connected with the Runcorn Co-operative Society,. Limited, was held in St. raul's Chambers, fer the pur- pose of considering questions affeetine, the Widnes mem- bers. Mr. Hughes was voted to the cnair, arid Mr. W. Wiliart was appointed secretary pro tem.—The Chairman said he felt very keenly the way iu which the Runcorn members bad treated and intended to treat the Widnes members. As one of the Revision Committee he coup. assure them they had a strong battle over the new rutile, t With them it was not a matter of W:dces v. Runcorn, e but how they could secure the interests of the society at large. (Hear, hear.) It, was in that spirit that they tried to frame the new rules, and while they were doing their utmost they heard many rep .rts, which had only, proved too true, that there was a body of members In Runcorn framing rules of their own, and that when the Revising Committee had finished their work it would . count as nothiea, because the rules they had framed would be thrown en one side, and the rules that had been arranged by the few members in Runcorn were to be taken instead. Be now thought Um it was time that, they in Widnes made some move towards separating from the Runcorn Society. The business :or the night was to make some arrangements for the future. Mr. A. Lloyd suggested that the principal revised rules should be read and contrasted with the old rues.—Mr. Willan read the old ruled, the main points being, with regard to the election of officers, that in future the board of directors, twelve in number, should be elected by ballot, three to be elected at each half-yearly meeting. Nominations were to be sent in (to any of the branches) • one month before the half-yearly meetingeand the vc•ting was to take place on a given day between the hours of a.m. and 9p.m., ballot boxes to be provided at each of the stores for the reception of the voting papers. Another rile stated that no member be allowed to be a director who had a blood relation in the employ, of the Society. Several members remaiked that the system of • voting proposed by the new rules was very much better ' than the system now in vogue; Mr. Poulsen observing that there was so much rowdyism at the half-yearly meetings now that the people did not know who they were voting fore—The Chairman : And no gentleman. can make an electioneering speech under the new rule. (Hear, Isear.)—Mr. Poulson said the were the point' * that the Runcorn committee trim takeen moth exception to. The question was, should they have a game of their own or should they go on indefinitely submitting to such. treatment as they had hitherto ieceived. They should ascertain whether they had any share, in the capital of the society and whether, if they left the society, could they secure their shere :lit; or, if not, should they leave the society and leave the Runcorn members the accumu- lated profits P Should they form a small committee to consider the wayis and means for separation P—Mr. Clarke thought time time bad come when they ouelit be start upon their own footing. The Runcorn members would not give them [airplay ; sod anyone who looked at the balance-sheet would see that Runcorn wasp:Mg down and Widnes was going up. He proposed that committee be formed —Mr. Shuttleworth thought they had better try and see if they could get • I some concessions from tke Runcorn rinemeersA be- fore starting a fresh society.—Mr. Willan said that some years ago a branch left the Dewsbury I society and tney took over their branch preittises. —Mr. Lloyd took it that those places were similar)/ situ- ated to Runcorn and Widnes. —Mr. Willan : Yea. The • reserve fund of their society belonged quee as much tO- ' the Widnes members as the -Rtincorn members, and they were entitled to their shies of Callow fillUes` ed. that a deputation be eppeieted to wait upon those at tint '• other side of the water and diemeas the matter. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

TEE "MODEL LODGING HOUSE" LICENSEE R 4 TROUBLE. At the Warrington Borough Police Court, yesterday, before Alderman Harrison, Alderman Wright, and Coun- cillor Roberts, Alfred Brouchemite was charged with having on the 2nd August, being the keeper of a lodging house, failed to remove solid and liquid filth before nine o'clock; he was also charged with not causing the floors and rooms and passages to be swept, and with not keep- ing the bedding and bed-clothes clean.—Defendant pleaded not guilty.—lnspector Norman stated that de- fendant was a common lodging-house keeper and kept the "Model Lodging House," Friars Green. On the morning in question about 11 o'clock witness visited defendant's premises and found the stairs and rooms very dirty. The utensils were not then emptied in accordance with the bye-laws. The bedding was dirty and there were only two clean beds out of the lot. Two deputies were in char,..se and they were asked about the offences and they said they could not get the clothes washed. A slopstone at the top of one of the landings down which the filth was poured emitted an abominable stench.—Assistant Inspector Powell corroborated. The defendant said he was not the keeper of the house and bad not been for a month, as it was in the hands of the mortgagee. He was not owner of the pronerty.—Mr. Norman said that the license was in Mr. Brouchemite's name:and he was therefore responsiole.—The bench fined defendant 108. and costs in each case and gave him a week to pay it in. Mr. Norman then made application to the magistrates for the license to be cancelled as the place was unfit tor lodgers. The licence was concelled. A LIVELY CIEARACTER.—SCENE IN COURT. On Monday morning at the Warrington Borough Police Court, Michael Cunningham, 38,James-st.,aod Elizabeth Ellen Fleming, Brick-St., a ticket-of-leave convict, were charged with frequenting Cockhedge-lane with felonious intent on Sattsrday night ; they were further charged with assaulting John Towey at the same time and place. —Fleming pleaded guilty.—John Towey stated that he was coming down Cockhedge-lane on Sunday night with his brother, when someone shouted and the female prisoner who was close by blamed him and ran at him and struck him.—His brother added, in corroborating, that Cunningham administered the kick which he had received.—Mr.flunt mentioned to the Bench that on the 9th March, 1893, the two defendants stood before the magistrates on a charge of robbery wi h violence and the evidence not being so conclusive against the male prisoner he was com• mitted for three months while Fleming was sent to the Assizes and sentenced to three years penal servitude. She was discharged on the 29th May this year on ticket- of-leave aod had to report herself monthly until March, 1896. She was sent to Cockhedge factory a short time ago in the hope that she would reform, and for some time she remained steady but of late had drifted into her old ways again. The two of them had got together again•and they were about the streets at night and were dangerous characters. He asked that the woman might be sent back to serve her time. They could very well spare her. Ihe magistrates adopted this course with regard to Fleming and she was being conducted out of Court when she rushed at Towey and screaming out a volume of oaths attempted to assault him but after a struggle with several of the police she was conducted to the cells, making use of foul language all the time.— Cunningham was sent to goal for two months' hard labour.

NEWTON PETTY SESSIONS.

SATUNDAY.—BeforeMPSATS. T. J. Gillesnie (Chairtuqn) J. W. Emmett, C. B. F. Borron, and S. S. Brown. ASSAULT ON A GlRL.—Thomas Wnreis, a butcher. of Golhorne, was fined 10s. and costs for nommittine an assault upon a girl named Scully, aged 14 rears, on July 21st. Mr. R. Stuart prosecuted, and Mr.. Percy Davies defended. DISORACEFUL notatotr OF FAMILY—James and Annie Jackson, 54, Twenty•eight•row, flaydock-lane. Anydock were charged with neglecting their six children on July 31st and divers other dates during the past six months. Mr. J. W. Knowles appeared on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C. Inspector McAteer said that on the above date he and P.C.Thiihron went to the defendant's house and found it in a, wretched condition. The children were covered with vermin and were insufficiently clad, and shoeless. The children were of ages ranging from 15 months to 14

years, and they appeared as though they had not been , washed for a long time. When questioned concerning the filthiness of the children, the female defendant re- plied that they were washed every week. The only furniture there was in the house was an old sofa, a chair, a broken bible, and a rickety be ;stead, the latter of which was cower d with some old sacking in a very insanitary condition. The house was unfit for a human being to live in. Witness had learned from the male de- fendant's employers, Messrs. R: Evans and Co., that his average earnings were £3 6e. per week, so that he earned suifi:iient to keep his home respectable.P.C. Thubron gave similar evidence and Dr. Roisiter, of Ray- dock, also gave evidence which showed the wretched condition under which the defendants and their children lived.—Mr. Knowles asked for a very severe punishment to be meted out to the defendants,whom he described as being worse than the beasts of the field for the latter did keep their young clean.—The defendants said that al- though their children were dirty,they were well fed mind expressed their sorrow at the condition in which they were found.—The magistrates retired to consider their decision, and on returning into the Court, Mr. Gillespie said that the case was a shocking one, and defendants would have to go to gaol for one month with hard , labour, the children in the meantime to bo taken to the Workhouse for maintenance.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 17, 1895.

MARRIAGE OF MR. A. D. SMITH AND

MISS CAVANAGH.

A very interesting event, locally, took place on Satur- day morning at ten o'clock, at St. Alban's Church, Bewsey-street, Warrington, when Miss Frances Mary Cavanagh, daughter of Mr. George James Cavanagh. of Horsemarket-street, and 83 Bewsey-street, WAS united in marriage to Mr. Alfred D. Smith, the head master of Trinity Board School, Shrewsbury. Miss Cavan igh was a member of the choir at St. Alban's Church, and as a singer she was very much in request in the town. She had also a good class in dancing and calisthenics for i a number of years. The clergy of St. Alban's the Revs. 1 Fathers Firth, Cody, and Craven have shown their ap- preciation of her services to the church,by presenting her with a very valuable writing desk,and the choir also pre- sented her with a handsome silver eggstand with spoons. Mr. Smith,the bridegroom,has held the position of bead- master at Shrewsbury Board School for ten years, and last week,a gathering of the teachers in connection with the school was held, when Mr. Smith was the recipient of congratulations and testimonials on the occasion of I his marriage. A presentation, which was made to him, took the form of a very handsome set of carvers, and I Mr. R. J. Howell, the ssnior assistant of the school, in making the presentation, spoke in very flattering term, of Mr. Smith's e.onnection with the school and his work during the past decade. He also spoke of ths esteem in which Mr. Smith was de;ervedly held by the parents of the children, and by the general body of the inhabitants of the distsict. as well as his popularity among his fel- low-teachers in the town, and lie (Mr. Howell) offered on behalf of the sub,cribers to the testimonial their most cordial congratulations to Mr, .Rinith on this interesting occasion.—Mr. Smith, in reply; expressed gratitude for I the kindness, mad the great pleasure to feel that his an. deavours to be of service had not been unappreciated by those whose esteem and good. will had been molt preci- ous to him. The toys attending the school also made ' a presentation of an aneroid barometer to Mr. Smith. At the ceremony on Saturday Morning, the otfieiating clergyman was the Rev. Father Cody. Ihe wedding party were driven to the church in two coaches and greys. The bride WAS tastefully attired in grey and creme silk dress and white hat, trimmed with ostrich feathers. I here were three bridesmaids, Miss Joanna Minog,ue, Miss Maggie Minogue, and Miss Agnes Stirrup, and they were attired in grey and green dresses with hats to match. The groomsman WAS Mr. Joseph William Cavanagh, the brother of the bride. After the conclus- ion of the interestiag cnremony, the organist. Mr, John Caldwell, played the well-known" Wedding March," as the party left the church. The wedding breakfast was held in the music room at the house of the bride, and there was a large number of friends present, including the Revs. Father Firth and Father Cody. During the course of the breakfast, the Rev. Father Firth rose and thanked Miss Cavaiiao.h for her many years service in the choir at St. Alban's and expressed his sorrow that she was leaving, although he congratulated her upon her marriage. He hoped that she wonld be present at the charity sermoos to September. Father Cody also made a humorous speech. After the wedding breakfast, the happy couple left Warrington for a tea days' visit to the Lake District. _ _ _ .

A large amber of handsome presents have been re- ceived and the following are the list in addition to those mentioned above :—Four o'clock tea service, set of knives, and table linen, Mrs. Cavanagh ; set of china, Mr. and Mrs. Alfred Cavanagh. Patricroft ; pair of glass and silver jam dishes and stand, Mrs. T. Morris, Bewsey Bridge ; handsome hand stitched table ninth, MOS Coil- brough ; ruby cream jug and sugar basin, Miss Agnes Stirrup set of dinner service, Miss Minogue ; tea urn, Mrs. Smith, Latchford ; bent iron tea kA.:.le, and lamp, Mrs. Pailip D )bson ; butter c -inter, Mr. and Mrs. G. Vernon; album,Mr.and Mrs. John Richardson,Roileston- street ; claret jog and glass,Mias Annie Marsh (servant) ; ruby and silver sugar basin, crumb tray, and brush,,Mrs. and the Misses Clare, Win wick-st. ; handsome pin Cushionand painted photo frame, Mrs. Efolland,Bewsey- st. ; scent jar, lady friend ; pickle dish, lady friend, &I

EUCALYPTUS -1N KITCHENS.

A few notes on that most wonderful of all trees, the fast-growing giant of the Australian fnrests,the eucalyp tus tree, is given in the " Health Messenger." Trio chemistry of its leaves and bark have of late received most careful attention at the hands of scientific fielr. and we may now safely count upon a far wider know- ledge of ite virtues spreading among all circles, says the journal. Indeed, most of us already know the eucalyp• tue characteristics as used in toilet soaps, disinfectants, and other preparation; of a similar description. But it is not so generall c understood that the eucalyptus pes- sessei really high-class disinfectant properties. Extracts from the leaves and hark, and in the form of OHg or resins, have the newer of oxidising obnoxiats and pesti- ferous substances, and by this chemical process of oxi- dation, splittinr, up the dangerous products of refuse into harmless gases, In other cases, the eucalyptus ex- tracts unite chemically or mechanically with one or more of the harmful matters, and by forming fresh com- pounds, effectually neutralise the evil effects that tnight arise from putrefaction. Thus, we ape that eucalyptus proparations are time disinfeceants, and not merely stink disguisers, as rnle too mine of the so-called dk- ' infectants prove to he. These preparations from the eucalpytus are now largely used in cempounding many of the best patent disinfectants in the market. Another valuable preperty of the eucalyptus is its antiseptic (or decay-arresting) qualities. It is found tha' If eucalyptus leaves are steeped in water, the water will remain perfectly pure and palatable for a lone time. in ;spite of exposure and heat. To a certain extent, it will also help to purify liquids tainted with organic mater. I.; has been proposed to take advantage of these qualities ; and use the extracts cr resins containing the essential oils and powerful alkaloids as disinfecting, mediums for water filtration. These extracts would 4st mixed either with the animal charcoal or earthly sponges of ordinary filters, or used as powders to be MiXel in the water, and precipitated with the impurities. Recently count Luigi Torelli, an Italian senator of some scientific attainpients, with great care carried out many ingenious experiments with eucalyptus leaves. He successfully proved th •jr remarkable powers for preserving and purifying water. He steeped a large variety .of harks and leaves in water. In some eases the additions had a bad effect, in others the water kept good for some time ; but in every case where eucalyptus leaves were used, the water remained palatable for art indefinite period. But he made other experiments which, we suspect,will prove more interest- ing to our readers, and should be of practical utility to many of our ttreign and colonial subscribers. The Count Luiei took some veal, killed during hot weather some twenty-four hours previously, and placed it in a.n open pan of water with a fox eucalyptus leaws. In a second open pan he placed more veal in water,but with- hut rucaleptus leavee or any other addition. After waiting seven•tgro ii aura, he found that the meat in the open pan with plain water tied become putrid, and communicated its impiinties to the water. Tire second open pan was next examined. The water was slightly coloured red and smelt of eucalyptus oil, tint otherwise it was perfew Iv drinkah'e and quite whraesome. The meat was quite fresh, though, of course, very pale in colour. It was cooked, and proved excellent. He next took a piece of veal which smelt rather " high." Thi, he carefully wrapped in a profusion of eunalyptus leaves and allowed to remain for three days on a plate. The leaves were then removed, they had imparted a slight odour and flavour to the meat, but, nevertheless, when duly cooked la Millanaise, it was pronounced delicious, and absolutely free from taint. Indeed,some enthusias- tic friends declared that the eucalyptus flavour was a decided improvement, ha which Count Luigi agreed, although he perhaps wis?ly adds, "de gustibus nen eat disgrutandum.''

TEIE SKIN IS CONIPOSED of three layers, a nd con- tains muscles, nerves, blood-YEssels, lymphatics, sebace- ous glands, sweat glands. etc., while the hair and nails are its appendages. It is a most complicated piece of machinery, and subject, like any other organ, to health and disease. To preserve the skin, scalp and hair, and to restore them to a condition of health when diseased, nothing is so agreeable or so speedily effective as concerns SOAP, assisted in the severer forms with mild- applications of COTICITRA (ointment), the great ekin cure.

BOWLING.

GOLBORNE SUBSCRIPTION CLUB V. ST. HELENS RECREA- TION Ctua.--The above clubs played their return match on Saturday at Goiborne, when the visiting team suc- ceeded in winning the gains by 34 points. At the con- clusion of the match both teams and a few visitors from St. Helens adjlurned to the Leh Arms Hotel, where a capital repast awited them, served in good style by Ser- geant-Maj Ir. and Mrs. Storror. Ample justice having been done to the good things provided, the cloth was re- moved and a nvist happy and convivial evening was spent, the proceedings being presided over by Dr. Dobb. Mr. Hartley, Sc. Helens, acted as vice-chairman. The usual toasts were drunk, and singing was freely in- dulged in. Dr. Mapei, and Mr. G. Holt, of St. Helens, contributed greatly to the evening's entertainment. Songs were also rendered by the following members :—Messrs. Dr. Dobb, A. Smith, John Worsley, E. Platt, and J. Cash. During the evening a presentation was made by the Chairman to Mr. Pitt for getting the lowest score. Mr. Pitt, he remarked, had been a members of the Golhorne club since its commencement (29 years), and he did not think he had ever before been beaten on his own green. Mr. Pitt, in responding, said he was very pleased to re- ceive a prize, but it would have been a greater pleasure to him had it been for the highest sc•re. WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL COMPANY.

IMPORTANT REPORT TO THE TOWN COUNCIL.

At the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Thursday evening the report of the Rivers' Committee upon their negotiations with the Manchester Ship Canal Company was taken into consideration. The report opened with an account of an interview between the Committee and the Directors of the Ship Canal Company, held on the 4th July. Councillor Roberts, the chairman of the Committee presided, sand intimated to the directors that the Corporation desired it to be understood that the interview was to be a final one, and asked the directors for their proposals with re- gard to the several matters in ispute. With regard to the Abstraction a Water, Mr. Bythell, on behalf of the Company, stated that the works'at Butchersfield Weir and Woolston were not yet completed, and asked that the matter might be allowed to stand over. With re- gard to the Dredging of the Ricer it was proposed on behalf of the Company that they would dredge a chan- nel within the defined limits, with the exception of that portion of the river above Latchford Weir, but including the portion of the old river from Walton Lock to the south end of the river diver- sion within a minimum width at the bottom of 50ft. With regard to the Warrington Dock the Company stated that they were without funds and unable therefore to construct the dock. They suggested that the Corpora- tion should themselves undertake the work, and that the Corporation should be allowed to receive the wharfage rates until the capital expended and the interest thereon was recouped. The Corporation declared their unwil- lingness to execute this work, and as an alternative,sug- gested that the Company should dredge the bed of the old river and construct a deep water lay-bye and a wharf for the use of the Warrington traffic. The Cor- poration further proposed that the Company should , remove the limit of 150 tons upon all vessels passing through the Walton Lock. At the termination :at the interview the Directors promised to submit their pro- posals in writing. The TOWN CLERK afterwards received the following letter dated Bth July, from Mr. A. H. Whitworth, secre- tary of the Ship Canal Company,—" I am instructed to comply with the request of the Rivers' Committee to write you with reference to the interview between the committee and a deputation of directors of the company which took place on Thursday last, the 4th instant. Matters were a little hurried at the last because some of the gentlemen present had to catch their trains, but the directors left with the impression that the Rivers' Com- mittee were willing to recommend the Corporation to agree to the following settlement :-1. That the term " bed and banks of the River Mersey" shall be defined as meaning a channel with a minimum width of fifty feet. 2. That the company shall maintain in the channel as so defined the statutory depth of Bft. below the level of low water of spring tides between the wes- tern boundary of the works of Monks. Hall and Co., at or near Bank Quay and Howley Lock, but not above the lock. 3. That the company in consideration of the Cor- poration agreeing to the foregoing definition of the obli- gations of the company under sub-section 14 of section 88 of the Company's Act of 1885 shall (a). Main- tain a channel with a minimum width of fifty feet and depth of eight feet below the level of low water of spring tides from the Walton Lock to where that channel joins the River Mersey near the new river diversion, although the Company believe and are advised that they were under no obligation to dredge, and are under na obligation to maintain such a channel. (b) Construct a wharf 300 feet long with deep water near the entrance to the Warrington Dock from the Ship Canal, at or near the site marked on the nlan received from the Warrington Corporation on the Bth May last. It is understood that the width of the channel at Warring'on Bridge may be an exception to the rule of fifty feet width, and will have to be settled by the engineers, having regard to the safety of the bridge. Reference was made to the ques- tion of modifying the tolls payable for the use oi the Walton Lock and Ship Canal, but, as explained to the I committee, so many questions are involved that the Directors cannot see their way to agreeing to the pro- posed modification." _ . . . . .

The Rivers' Committee after considering the above letter reaolved that the Comoany bo ask,d :-1. To dredge the river in front of all existing and future wharfs to the depth of 8 feet. 2. To reduce the tolls leviable upon Warrington traffic from 3d. and 4d. to 61. and 3d., or as an alternative to dredge the bed of the old river at Wilderspool to a depth sufficient for Warrington traffic. 3. To remove the limit of 150 tons upon all vessels passing through the Walton Lock. The channel of 50 feet referred to in the above letter is understood to be a channel with a minimum width of 50 feet at the bottom.

The following letter was sent to the Company by the Town Clerk in reply to their letter of the Bth July : " Your letter of the Bth instant was fully considered by my committee yesterday, and lain instructed to inform you that the cemmittee d-4 not consider the terms pro• posed by you sufficient to justify them in advising a settlement of the matters referred to in your letter. The committee wish me to remind you that the terms that may be agreed upon will have to be submitted to the

traders and manufacturers of the Borough, and unless I they are able to show something in the nature of a quid pro quo they fear there will be little chance of obtaining their assent or the approval of the Council. The Com- mittee are under a promise to submit the proposed terms of settlement to the Council at its next meeting, and! th .y are exceedingly anxious to place before the Coun- cil reasonable proposals for the complete settlement of the several questions between us. It would be greatly to the advantage of your Company to he relieved from the obligation to dredge the whole width of the river, and to construct the Warrington Dock, and the only proposal you make in consideration of these matters is to construct a wharf 300 feet long on the Canal. Assuming that your Company are under no obligation to dredge the river from the Walton Lock to the new river diversion, and which work the Corporation are advised your Company are bound to execute, the prAposed relinquishment of the dredging of the length of river above Howley Lock is more than an equivalent. The Committee desire me to point out that the proposed chaneel is to have a mini- mum width of fifty feet at the bottom. They ask your Company to undertake :-I.—Tn dredge the river in front of all existing and future wharves within the pro- posed limits. 2.—To remove the limit of 150 tons upon all vessels passing through Walton Lock. 3.-To reduce the tolls of 4d. and 13d. referred to in Section 88 to 3d. and 6d. respectively, or 89 an alternative to dredge the bed of the old river at Wilderspool. and to construct a wharf In a position to be agreed upon by the engineers suitable and sufficient for Warrington traffic. With every desire to assist the Canal Company, the Corpora- tion feel that they are dealing with important interests which have been protected or secured to the Borough at great cost, interests a failure to protect which would in- volve the Corporation in a breach of their obligations, not only to the inhabitants, but also to the traders and manufacturers of the Borough, whom the Corporation are bound to consider. It is hoped that your Company will see their way to accept the above moderate pro- posals of the Corporation." The next letter from Mr. Whitworth to the Town Clerk

dated 10th August, reads as follows.:::‘' Th-e-Di-t:e-C"tc;r9 have carefully considered your letter of the 26th ultimo, in which you state that your committee are of opinion that the terms set forth in my letter of the Bth idem are not sufficient concessions for modifying the dredging obligation as suggested, and for relieving the Company of the obligation to construct the Warrington dock. The Directors still think that the proposed terms were, con- siding all the circumstances, not unreasonable ; hut in order to assist you in carrying out the desired speedy settlement, they will so far meet the views of your com mittee as to agree to the following modifications of their proposals as made by letter on July Bth 1. The Company will undertake to dredge not only in front of the existing wharves on the river, but also in front of any future wharves which may be constructed within five years from this date, on the North side of the River Mersey between the dredging limit at Bank Quay and the Howley Lock, and to dredge in front of any other wharves at the expense of the owners thereof, which may be erected after the expiry of the five years within the same limits on the North side, provided the owners of all such wharves, existing and future, indemnify the Company against any damage to their wharves which may arise from such dredging, and pro- vided that the Company shall only dredge in front of the wharves that are in uSe and then only the portions of such wharves that are actually required for traffic pur- poses. Ido not suppose that the owner of any wharf would wish us to dredge in front of his wharf when he did not want to use it.

2. The tolls in force for the use Qf the Ship Cana and the Walton Lock are so low that the Company, hav- ing regard to the large amount of money spent on the construction of the Ship Canal, think that no reduction ought to he made, hut they will nevertheless if obso- letely necessa y reduce the tolls to 3d. and 6d. respec- tively.

3. The Company will he prepared to remove the limit of 150 tons referred to in sub-section 2of Section 88 of the 1885 Act for vessels conveying goods outside the river Mersey and to raise the limit to 250 tons in respect of vessels conveying goods to and from the river Mersey, vitt Walton Lock.

The understanding is as you state that the channel is to have a minimum width of 50 feet at the bottom. It is to be dredged so as to have a depth of 8 feet of water maintained at low water of spring tides, and the level of low water at Warrington Bridge is to be taken at 10 feet above O.D.

_ In reply to a verbal inquiry I am directed to say that the Directors agree that your Corporation shall reserve the power to construct the Warrington Dock at the ex- pense of the Corporation on the bias.of the terms dis- cussed some time ago, that is to say that the Corpora- tion shall rec.ive the wharfage rates levied for the use of the Dock until the cost thereof with interest is cc- Cooped, when the Dock will become the property of the Ship Canal Company. It iq understood that the Corporation will waive all penalties for delay in completing the dredging.

The final result of the negotiations, was the Pans' by the Rivers Committee of the following resolution $ •-• " That subject to the channel above reterred to being dredged to a depth of 8 feet at low water a spring tides,. , and to the construction,or one or more turning Places in such channel, the above terms be agreed to and mitted to the Council."

TOWN CLERK.

On the minutes of the Health Committee which were submitted to the Warrington Town Coun- cil at its meeting on Thursday night the following resolution is recorded, " That, in the opinion of this Committee it is desirable in the interests of public health that a public abattoir should be established at an early date." In view of the probable discussion upon this question each member of the Council has been sup- plied with an extract from the annual report of the Medical Officer of Health upon the subject of the slaughter-houses of the town, and also a report of the Town Clerk upon the position of the Corpora- tion with regard to slaughter-houses. In his report the Medical Officer mentions that there are 28 slaughter-houses lU the borough, 25 of them being in use. "Twenty-three are in close contiguity with dwelling-houses. Four are connected with licensed pre- mises, and 12 are contained within the premises of the owner or oecupier. They are governed by certain by- laws, but the result of my inspection shows that there is to some extent a disregard of their observance. The pro- vision of lairages is in most slaughter-houses very inade- quate, and there are many defects in the old slaughter- houses, such as (a) an insufficient supply of water, (I) absence of suitable ventilation, (e) the storage of animal refuse, garbage, and filth, giving rise to offensive em- anations. lam satisfied from my frequent visits that nothing like a systematic daily removal of filth ii car ried out by the butchers of the town. This laxity of conduct does not arise from any want of supervision,but from the fact that anything like an adequate inspection of the slaughter-houses by our present staff of inspectors is an impossibility." The Medical Officer adds that the only effectual way of carrying out the proper slaughter- ing of animals is by the establishment of a public abattoir, and he enumerates the following benefits which an abattoir would confer upon the public :—(1) It is the only means' of removing the continually recurring nuisances which are frequent in the slaughter-houses of the town. (2) A better quality of meat would be obtained, and all diseased and questionable meat would be at once easily discovered by constant supervision. (3; It would also be a financial benefit to the town, for wherever abattoirs have been established they have been fairly successful. From returns published the one at Bradford pays 6 percent., the Manchester one not less than 4 per cent., and at Birkenhead 5 per cen . is being realized. In the close of his report upon the legal position of the Corporation with regard to slaughter-houses the Town Clerk says : "The local Act cf 1851 specially empowers the Corporation to erect slaughter-houses and fix tolls for the use of them. It is therefore quite clear that upon the Corporation erecting slaughter-houses and after giving the requisite notice thereof it will be unlawful to slaughter in any slaughter-house erected or first used since 1854. With regard to slaughter-houses which were in use and occupation before and at the time of the passing of the special Act, and which were registered by the Corpora- tion within the three months allowed by the Act it must not be presumed that all such slaughter-houses may continue to be used after the erection of slaughter- houses. It is not sufficient that such places were in use and occupation before and at the time of the passing of the special Act,and which entitled them,in the first place to be registered, it is essential that they shall have been " continued in use ever since. I am informed that there are several buildings have ceased to be used as slaughter-houses ' since they were registered as such, and others that have not been continued in use ever since they were regis- tered, but which are now in use, having been licenqed by the Corporation upon the application of the owner or occupiers to be allowed to resume the use of them. It follows that all such slaughter-houses may be closed as though they had been erected or first used since the special Act was passed." A table compiled by Inspector Norman shows the number of animals slaughtered within the borough each week to be-123 cows, 44 calves, 344 sheep, and 146 pigs. The tolls fixed by the Act for the use of slaughter houses are as follows—cow, bullock, &c., Is.; calf, 6d.; sheep, 3d. ; pig, 4d. The Borough Surveyor supplies an approximate estimate of the cost of erecting a pubic abattoir as follows : land, £l,OOO ; buildings, £5,000 ; fittings and machinery, £BOO ; sundries, £2OO ; total, £7,000. The Town Clerk adds that " Assuming that the table compiled by the Inspector of Nuisances as to the number of animals slaughtered within the borough is correct,l am of opinion that a public abattoir would be about self-supporting."

A POLICEMAN'S ROMANCE.

Police-constable Taylor's romance is over. For four- teen years he zealously perambulated his beat in Bermondsey, gaining the esteem of his superior officers and the respect of all wha knew him except those who broke the law, the police regulations, and the public peace. Suddenly (says the " Daily Telegraph ") some tempter whispered into his ear that he was the long lost heir to a vast fortune, consisting of accumulated sums amounting to £120,000 in golden sovereigns, and largo estates in the counties of Surrey and Berks. Taylor believed the stories that were told him, resigned his place in the force, with the weekly salary of 355. attach- ing to it, laid aside his helmet and his truncheon for ever as he supposed, and sallied forth to enjoy his wealth. When his good fortune became kGown, he received nearly two thousand letters of congratulation, mostly frem people of whose names he was ignorant, but who claimed acquaintance with him in childhood, and hoped they would not be forgotten. But when the constable I had spent his savings in making inquiries about his " property," he found that, like most stories of long-105t 1 heirs, the information on which he had acted was all wrong, and the gold and the estates simply myths. A number of Bermondsey people who had known him as a faithful policeman, subscribed a small sum to tide him over the difficulties in which he found himself, and also petitioned the Commissioner at Scotland Yard to rein- state him in the force. The request has at length been granted, and on Sunday Taylor once more doinned the helmet and uniform associated with the fourteen hap- piest years of his life. Ells dream of wealth, beyond the regulation pension, has gone for ever.

CURIOSITIES OF COOKING.

In a French cookery book of the fourteenth century one is told how to dress a hedgehog to make it taste like a chicken. "How to make a fat lamb of a pig" sounds ingenious, but rather a waste of energy, and it is amus- ing to find a recipe for good coffee start unabashedly with "Take a quart of horse beans." As often as not, I directions in these ancient manuals are extremely vague, 1 and the cook is told in a lordly way to "take pigs," or pigeons, or "an indifferent quantity of butter." One is struck by the number of herbs which our great-grand- mothers used, and which the present generation despise. Who eats chopped primrose !elves at the present day, or broom buds pickled in vinegar, or clovegillifiowers soused in claret, that have stood for a month in the sun? Such poetic feeding is not for us! "To make a Fool "requires two quarts of cream and twelve eggs ; we have known one made in A loss expen- sive manner. As delicacies the Greeks ate young foxes caught in the autumn, rebins, and sparrows, and certain finds of fish snared by moonlight. There is a scrap of an old Greek cornedv *says the " ii,•lden Penny," in which a cook b ,asts of fryin, a fi,ll .i.) exquisitely that it threw Min grateful loi;ks from the pan. A famous Greek dish was the Trojan uh,,,half of it boiled and the other half roasted. , It was stuffed with eggs, ortolans, and thrwhes. Tee Romans ate snails—giants, monsters—fattened until their shells held an incredible amount of snail. The ' kettle in a high class Roman kitchen was often shaped like an elephant's head ; the water was pmred through the trunk. The grid iron might be a huge silver spider or a skeleton fish.

UTILISING NIAGARA.

Why should the waters of Niagara be wasted ? They maze an imposing and picturesque display, no doubt, but the Yankee wants to train them in the ways of prac- ticality. "And you bet your bottom dollar l'll do it," , says Uncle Sam. The idea has been under discussion for years. In 1870, as Mr. Silvanus P. Thompson recalls in the "Saturday Review," Sir William Siemens, dealing ! with the utilisation of natural forces, made the sugges- tion that electricity might intervene as the means of transmitting to distant places power derived from the Falls. Lord Kelvin dealt further with the plan before a Parliamentary Committee. That was all very well, the theory was inviting enough, but its practical realisation baffled the Columbians. Paper schemes were flying about, as Mr. Thompson says, and engineers talked vaguely of millions of horse-power. " What was definitely known was that if two dynamos, even those Of the kind used for elsctric lighting, placed at two distant points, were joined together by suitable lines of copper-wire into a circuit, power could be transmitted between them by the electric current which circulated from one to the other. Set a steam-engine or a turbihe to turn one of the two dynamos, the other at the distant point would forthwith revolve, and would give out as mechanical power a very large percentage of the power supplied at the generating station. The stationary electric wi.,e takes the place of the running rope or twirling shaft, with an obvious economy whenever the distance of transmission exceeds a few yards." In Switzerland a good deal was done in the development of this work of electric transmission, and some half a-dozen years ago a company of New York capitalists fairly tackled the question as it concerned Niagara. They studied it from the engineering, fioancial,and commercial point of view, and then faced the business of rights and companies. A Land Devetopment Company built a manufacturing village, a Niagara Falls Power Company undertook the electric distribution of power, a Contract Construction Company, directed the engineering work. A Roving Commission collected expert opinion. At this stage, says Mr. Thompson occurred the one. discreditable episode the savour of which will ever cling about the undertaking. Acting on the best of advice,it was decided to fix the size of the electric generators at 5,000 horse-power each, and to mount them,as the Swiss engineers were doing, on vertical shafts going down to the turbines. Plans of machinery were then invited by the Cataract Company from the leading manufacturers and designers of Europe, with intimations which, how- ever speciously conveyed. were destined to be rudely dispelled. For when some twenty sets of designs, many of which were pronounced by the Contract Company's own engineers to be " Extremely good," had been framed with infinite pains, the Contract Company coolly rejected all, and directed their engineers to pre- pare "their own" de3igni. All the excuse ever offered i)r this ungenerous picking of the brains of others wa , 'the increased cost of using European designs, owing to the high tariff in America, and owing to transport! "1 Early in the present year the first of the new 5,000 horse- power electric generators was delivered. The first three turbines had already been placed by a Pedladelphia firm at the bottom of the great vertical shafts. A few days ago regular ruuning began, and will continue until fully 50,000 horse-power is supplied. The first consumers are an aluminium company ; but several acres of factories are already built in the new town to receive the supply of power. One factory alone wants 3,300 horse-power, an- other 1,000, a third 1,500. Even when 100,000 horse- power shall have been taken from the great river, the difference to the eye in the quantity of water will be im- perceptible. Such is America's latest big project,which, it is confidently stated, possesses all the elements of success.

Manufactured only at 78, NEW OXFORD STREET, LONDI

ROYAL ANIMAL MEDICINE MANUFACTORY.

MUSIC FOR THE PEOPLE.

Sir,—Now that the band seoson is drawing to a close, I should,as one who is deeply interested in the playing of the bands in the Park, like to suggest that something should be done to encourage the bands in their good and useful work. Could not the Band Association see their way clear to give a cup to be contested for by the local bands—for instance, a cup something similar to one of those given to the local Football Junior teams ? If this could be done I feel confident it would be the means ofl bringing up the bands to a better state of efficiency. am not in any way complaining of the playing, but I think there is room for a great improvement. With the number of bands in Warrington I think if till above suggestion could be acted upon it would stimulate the members to attend practice all the year round, as I find by inquiry that there are a number of bands who only have pra,.:tice in the summer season, with the exception of a little practice at Christmas time. Not being in any band myself but a lover of good muiic, I think with the material there is in Warrington we ought to be able to hold our own with other contesting bands, ssch as Bosses, Wyke, Dyke, Kingston, Denton, Mossley and others. Hoping that an abler pen than mine will take up the subject.—Youra truly, HARMONY. Warrington, August 9th, 1895*
