WARRINGTON.

LOYAL ORDER OF ANCIENT SHEPHERDS.

The members of the " Warrington Juvenile Burial Society," and Widows and Orphans' Fund of the above-named Order assembled at the British Workman Public-house, Horsemarket-st , on Mon- day, to hold their 24th annual meeting in connec- tion with the Juvenile Society and the 40th annual meeting of the Widows and Orphans' Fund. The members belonging to bath funds responded to the summons convening the meeting to a very large ex- tent,in fact to a larger extent than had been the case for some previous years. The meeting was presided over by the President, P.P.C.S. William Gleave,who was supported by the vice-President, P C S. Thomas Duckworth. The Chairman' in the course of his address, alluded to the work of the past year, and said it bad been an eventful year for the Order and full of much encouragement, and he thought was highly gratifying to them all to find that the efforts made to improve their financial position had re- sulted in the increase of capital during the year to such an extent as would suprise even the most san- guine amongst them. The general secretary, P.P.C.S. Lawrence Weir, in submitting the annual balance sheet for the year showed a gain upon the year's accounts for the Juvenile Society of £145 lls. 91 . thus making the total worth of the Society £1,374 17s. lid. and a total membership of 1,147. The report further showed that the Widows and Orphans' fund had made a gain of C 8 155.2 d. upon the years transactions, and a total worth of £l9l , is. 5d He said the balance for the Juvenile Society had not been 'gained without its difficul- ties. They had, like other collecting Societies, had to contend with the depression in trade, and it had only been achieved by the perseverence of the col- lectors who he believed were of sterling value to the society. He further reviewed the pro- gress the society had made for some years back,and stated that year after year they bad made a steady gain, in fact the balance had never been on the reverse side and in taking the 5 years that had just passed they showed a nett gain of £519 6. W. and an increase of membership of 40) for that period, and he considered this augured well for the future of the society. The auditors, P.P.C.S. Peter Edgar and John Jones, in giving their report compli- mented the members rpon the satisfactory manner they bad found the accounts of both societies, and assured the members that so long as such diligence was excercised by those in charge of mattersin that direction the society must continue to prosper.— The election oZ officers and committee next took place. P.O.S. Thomas Duckworth was unanimously elected President for the ensuing year,and D.P.C.S. William Eckersley, vice president. The following officers were also appointed: general secretary. P.P.C.S. Lawrence Weir; treasurer, P.P.C.S. James Parr ; trustees, Bros. William Holmes, Peter Itkigar, and William Ecicersley ; auditors, Bros. Peter Edgar and John Jones; committee, Bros. John Jones, Joseph Whitlow' Joseph Sykes William Broaay, Thornss Miller, Samuel Bradshaw, William Hall, Thomas Warburtor, Wiliam Knowles, Jeffery Benion, Edward Cliff', George Howard, David Harris, henry Warburton. and James Gresty. The remainder of the agenda paper was completed. It contained motions of an important character which canard very lively discussion. The orineipal speakers who took part were Bros. Jam, es Perr,Peter Edgar, Juffery Benion, Fred Massey,Pet er Ki rk ha ro, Simnel Blythe, Thomas Miller. A vote of thanks was accorded to P.P.C.S. William Gleuve, the re- tiring president, for his services during the past year and several members spoke in very high terms of the manner in which he had discharged .those duties. Bro. W. Gleave suitably responded, after which the meting terminated. THE REGISISATION OF TRISH VOTERS IN WARRINGTON.

, The usual weekly meeting of the Leagne of the Cross. took place on Sunday evening, iu the League • Hall, Bewsey-street, Mr. John Mullins, president, in the chair. Mr. Mullins in his address referred to ' the coming revision of the list of voters. He said , that the estimated number of Irish people in War- rington amounted to 9,000, and that according to . the average number of voters, which is one in seven of the total population, the Irish vote should be • 1,285. But,. be lamentable fact if, that the Irish vote is less than 600. He went on to show by a careful analysis of the whole electorate, that the proportion of voters (exclusiveof the Irish element) was one in four of the whole of the population; whilst the proportion of voters, held by the Irish, was only one in eighteen. The President concluded a lengthy address by exhorting the Irish people of Warrington to exert themselves, and not to rest until they were properly represented on the regis- ter. He laid particular stress on the necossity of making a personal claim. Every man entitled to he on tbe register should, he said, see after it himself, and not leave this important business in the care of anyone. He said it was all very well for people during the excitement of election time, to shout and to become interested, but now is the time to be interested. The battle is to be fought it* and not at election times only. The President, after saying everything that could be said in order to rouse the Irish people from the lethargy which seeps to hold themdown on this fubjact, resumed his 'seat after calling.on the Vice-President toitddress the meet- ing. .dr. J. C. Dalton, the vice-president, said the rank and rile of the Irish people in Warrington were not 140 much to blame, as were the leaders of Irish opinion in the town. There seemed to be a spirit of inertness existing amoog the leaders, which showed that they were destitute of the power of moving themselves in this important matter, and so long as this state of things was allowed to continue they need not blame the people for their indifference, tie advocated the coming together of the Irish people, and the forming of a compact body of workers, pledged lovingly to carry this work on to a succestul issue. Tee rest of Mr. Dalton's address dealt with the evils of in- temperance generally, but more especially of the terrible havoc it was making amongst the Irish people. He concluded by exhorting the members of the League to he faithful to their religious duties, as it was their only hope of preservation. The Rev. Father Ryan, of the Church of the Sacred Heart, Bank Quay, administered tli, pledge of total abstinence to six men, and counselled them to be it faithful to their pledge, and as a means to that end obe faithful to their monthly communion. It is a very pleasant feature of these meetings of the League, that they are not only well attended, but seem to be a channel of very profitable information and instruction for the people.

JOHN'S CHURCH SUNDAY SCHOOL, The annual treat to the scholars attending the above Sunday School took place on Saturday after- noon. The chilaren, to the number of about 1,100 mustered at the church at one o'clock,where a short service washeld by the Rev. T. R. Matthews, vicar. At the conclusion they formed in procession, and headed by the Earlestown prize band, with the Re- formatory school band heading the boys, they made a tour of the principal streets of the town, arriving at the Newton race course grand stand about four o'clock. Tea was served shortly after arrival in the upper and lower rooms of the stand, to which the scholars did full justice. Soon after tea rain began to fall, which put outdoor games out of the question and many of the children went home, whilst others remained and enjoyed themselves romping about, the capacious stand. To make up for the disappoint- ment, the Earlestown band attended at the race coarse on Monday,and besides dancing for the elders various games were indulged in until dark. FIRE AT NEWTON. About five minutes past 12 on Wednesday Morn- ing, P.C. Jones was passing along High-street, New- ton, when he observed the window curtains and drapery in the parlour window of 121, High-street, occupied by Mr. Thomas Mayor, to be. in flames. He aroused the inmates, and together with Mr. Mayor he carried buckets of water from the back kitchen, and succeeded in extinguishing the flames. It appears one of Mi. Mayor's daughters about 10 minutes previous to the discovery, had been into the room,and used a lighted match for the purpose of finding a reel of sewing cotton, which was amongst some cloth cuttings in a newspaper under the window, and it is supposed that a por- tion of the match fell amongst the cloth and crowd the tire, and if P.C. Jones had not seen it when he did, the damage would have been considerable. As it was, it was estimated at. between £2 and £3. SONS OF Txmisitnarrcx. The usual forLnightly meeting in connection with the Samuel Morley Section of Cadets was held on , Wednesday at seven o'clock in the Co-operative Hall. Sister Ethel Pearson, W.A. presided. The usual business was gone through The adult, mem• hers met in the same room at eight o'clock, Bro. John Pickett, W.P.. presided. After opening, a dis- cussion was entered into by the membsre on sick members as to their duties during sickness, one special case was mentioned and special help was promised if needed, thus showing the Sons of Tem- perance do not forget their more unfortunate brethren in time of need, exemplifying one of the cardinal principles øf the order "charity" by ex- tending a helping hand to each other. Several matters of interest to the order were mentioned and talked over, and a very pleasant meeting closed about 945.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 17, 1895 EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON. NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL 1.0.G.T. (EBENEZER LODGE). The usual weekly session ot the Earlestown Eb- enezer Lodge was held in the Co-operative Hall on ' fuesday evening. The sessson was presided over by Bro.W.Hatton,C.T., and one candidate was admitted to membership. The programme of the evening was a 'Fruit banquet," Bros. Slorri and W. Brown being responsible. A choice selection of lruit was distributed to the members present, and a very en- joyable evening was spent. Thanks were tendered to Bros. Morris and Brown for their efforts in mak- ing the meeting successful. NEWTON FAIR. The August cattle lair was held at Newton on Monday and Tuesday. The first day was devoted to the sale of cows of which there was a very good show, many considering it the best held for the past ten years. The cattle changed hands freely, good milkers fetching £2O, while as much as £25 was realised. On Tuesday the horse fair was heti, c;,cl there was a good show of horses, both in num- ber and quality. heavy cart horses brought £5O eacli,whilst the lighter class changed hands at from £35 to £4O. Cobs realised £2O to £25 each. Busi- ness was good, and there were plenty of buyers from Manchester, Liverpool and Wales. Mr. W. Ronayne, a local dealer, had a good show Of cobs for which a ready sale was effected.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held on Monthly atternoon in the Earlestown Town Hall. Mr.C. B. F. Borrori, J.P.. C.C., presided, and there were also present :—Messrs. J. Simpson, W. Beattie, F. J. Woods, T. Ormond, W. Gilmour, J. W. Emmett, J.P., P. Mc- W. Sloe, E. T. Evans, Rev. E. W. W. Kaye, J. E. Worsley (law clerk), and R. Brierley (clerk and sur- veyor).

Before the business or the THE LATE COLONEL MCCORCIIIODALL Council was entered upon, the Chairman said that since the last meeting of the Council a gentleman had passed away whose loss was regretted and who for about half a century had resided in that neighbourhood. He referred to Colonel McCorquodale, who was as they were all aware a very deeply prominent figure in that district both in private and public life. He formed one of those who originally in- itiated the formation of the Newton Improvement Com- missioners. They would find his name as being one who took part in the capacity of Commissioner for over 40 reams° that in his public life he had served that consti- iiiency and had devoted his well-known business capa- city to the benefit of the district. Looking back for so long a time they must all feel that the public had lost a very valuable and earnest servant, and that his place in that district would be very difficult to fill. He would add no more but ask the Council to pass the following resolution :—" That this Council &sites to express its regret with which it has heard of the death of Colonel McCorquodale, who was one of the original Improve- ment Commissioners nominated by the Newton District Improvement Act in 1855, and who was from that time until its extinction last year, a most valuable member ef that Board of which he had sat as chairman for over a quarter of a century ; and that this Council desires further to express its appreciation of the valuable ser- vices rendered by Colonel McCorquodale to the &strict in many other capacities ; also that a copy of this r.on• lution be pissed under the common seal of the Council and forwarded to Mrs. McCorquodale with an expres- sion of sincere sympathy and conglolenco of the Council with Mrs. McCorquodale and family for the loss they had sustained." Mr. Emmett seconded .the resolution. He said that he could only reiterate what the resolution had stated. The public had lost a very valuable friend, and he could speak from long experience. He had had the pleasure of sitting at that Beam with Colonel McCorquodale for over a quarter of a century, and he had never met a more perfect gentleman. Be was a man who always poured oil on the troubled waters, not only when on the Board, but elsewhere. He regretted that he had to second the resolution, in one sense, because Colonel McCorquodale had passed away. Mr. Gilmonr, as one of the-yeungeermembers on the Board,but one of the oldest inhabitants of the neighbour- hood, supported the resolution. He said that he had at various times gone on deputations to Colonel McCorquo- dale, on various subjects, and he had always received from him the greatest cordiality and courtesy. As the Chairman had already said, the whole neighbourhood would deeply deplore his loss. He himself deeply regretted that in the, order of Providence the Colonel had been taken away, and he was sure that what had been said was not mere sentiment, but that they hon- estly believed it. . The Law Clerk on behalf of himself and the officials of the Council, expressed his appreciation of the resolution which had been proposed. He could assure them that during the time he had served there as law clerk,a period fast approaching.2o years, there was never a time when Colonel McCorquedale's management of that Board had not been conspiciuous for its ability and courtesy,and for, its keen grasp at the business which was presented to the Board.. His rule there was agreeable to the officials and was of real benefit to the district, for the various things which he promoted. He -(tbe law clerk) had a melan- chol), pleasure in endorsing everything which had been said.'

THE'OTICHSEERS' QUESTION AGAIN. TRH ..,MINGTES OF THH

LAST MEETING REJECTEE.

The Clerk then read the minutes of the last meeting of the Council. Before they were formally put to the Council

Mr. Gilmour questioned the correctness of the Minute with reference to the discussion of his motion as to the overseers at the last meeting. He said that the Clerk made it appear that he (Mr. Gilmour) was incorrect. Mr. Ormand also called this minute in question. " • Mr. Emmett said that this matter ought to be cleared up before the minutes were passed. The Law Clerk ought to say whether the Council could appoint more than two overseers Of not. •

The Law Clerk said that he thought that the explana- tion he gave at the last meeting would have satisfied Mr. Gilmour. ,The circular which had been istued tram the Local Government Board recommended that for the present year, the number of overseers appointed should be the same number as had hitherto been appointed. The number hitherto appointed in that district had been two, and he, therefore, advised them to elect two. When the questiorr came up for discussion the next time, there was plenty of time to discuss the matter. Mr. Ormand said the misfortune was that the circu- lar had not been produced. The question waa not a legal one, and was one which the members of the Board could either take up er leave alone. Mr. Emmett contended that the Llw Clerk still adhered to his former statement that only two could be appointed. Mr. Slee said that the question was perfectly clear to him. They could appoint two, three, or four,but it was advisable that they should remain as they were for 12 months. _ _

Itlr.Emmett eaid that the Law Clerk ought to say that ho was mistaken in the advice he had given.

Mr. McMahon said that he had distinctly asked Mr. Womlev, before the agitation upon this question com- meneed, how many overseers could be appointed, and Mr.Worsley had said two and no more. The Law Clerk was over-ruling an Act of Parliament. The Chairman observed that it did not matter to a single individual whether there were two, three, or four appointed. In his view, the Law Clerk advised them to make no alteration upon what had teen previously done, and he thought that that was the right course to take. Tie minutes were then put to the Council, and the following was the result of the voting. For the passiqg of the minutes : the Chairman, Messrs. Wood,Simpson, glee, and Kaye, 5; against : Messrs. Emmett, McMahon, Gilmour, Beattie, Evans, and Ormand, 6. The minutes were therefore rejected. The Clerk: What are you going to do now?

The Chairman,: Those who opposed the minutes ought to move a resolution showing where they aro innecurlite.

The Clerk : Is there any dignity in these proceed- ines ? •

The Chairman aaid that it was only fair to the Law Clerk that any error which he made ought to be moved in the form of an amendment. It was not right toat the gentlemen should vote against the minutes without showing in what sense they were inaccurate._

, , It was finally decided to proceed with the business of the meeting. . HIGHWAYS kC. COMMITTEE. - .

LECTURE ON NEWFOUNDLAND On Tuesday evening the Rev. T. J. Newman of St. John's, Newfoundland, and formerly of Earlestown, lectured in the Wesleyan Chapel, Barlestown, on " England's Oldest Colony ; Newfoundland." Mr. Thomas Ormand presided, but there was only a scanty audience. The Chairman said he felt sure they would accord a hearty welcome to their -old esteemed friend, Mr. Newman, and that he would be glad to nhake hands with the friends of the past, and those he was associated with in the Sunday school and the church. Although Mr. Newman could not claim a residential qualification in Earle— town, he claimed a warm corner in the hears of many in the town. (Applause.) —Mr. Newman said he felt it a pleasure to address them on the subject of England's oldest c.dony. They were all English out there, and he did not think Her Majesty had ny more loyal subjects than she had there. Before India or the West Indies appeared on our list, and before any missionaries were sent off by the Wesleyan Church, Newfoundland, had her first missionary in Lawrence Copford. So little seemed to he known of this island that strange ideas pre. veiled in the minds of some respecting wctinags, asTheit. The general impression which rev one expressed it, that it was a land of td.h, frog and dog. The island was situated on the North side of the entrance to the gulf of St. Lawrence, its circuit being estimated at 1.000 miles, and the cost was broken up into beautiful bays, which were noted for their charming scenery. Notre Dame, which was a French possession, had been fitly descrihtiflS the garden of Newfoundland. The publication .of " Newfoundland illustrated " had helped to dispel the views in regard to that unknown island. The soil was rich, particularly in the valleys,and on the shores of tho lakes, the forests in Rome parts were very dense.‘and all sorts of wild flowers and fruit trees abound. There were no reptiles in New- foundland ; they had neither frogs, toads, nor lizards, and :hey were proud they had got none. MB. WILSON BARRETT AT ST. HELENS.

GOOD TEMPLARS CONVENTION.

The half-yearly session of No. 1 Convention, S.W. Lancashire, was held on Saturday, in the Good Templars Hall, Hindley. The meeting was opened at 3.45 p.m., by Bro. Q.M. Sergeant J. W. Roberts, C.C.T., of War- rington, presiding. There was a good attendance of representatives from lodges in Warrington, Earlestown, Prescct, St. Helens, Ashton, Wigan, Tyldesley and Hind- ley. The usual press and credential committees were appointed, after which the reports of officers were received. Bro. Roberts, C.C.T., in an interesting report offered some useful suggestions for the improvement of the Order in the Convention area. Jiro. E. Newman, C. secretary, had received reports from some of the lodges, several showing a small decrease in numbers. He hoped, however, that those yet to report would indicate an improvement. The Parliamentary Election had to some extent interfered with the general work of the Order, but he trusted that now that was over the lodges would stir up and during the coming sessions materially improve their positions. —Bro. Francis, C.E. Superintendent,gave a report on electoral action which, as altready indicated, had received considerable attention dur- ing the past qaarter, but the result had not been tar satisfactory as was desired.— The financial report read by Bro. George Tomlin, C. Treas. of Warrington, showed a good balance in hand. Several matters atf, ming the internal working of the Convention received consideration, after which the ! officers for the ensuing half-year were duly elected C.C.T. Bro. Q.M. Sergt. Roberts, Warrington ; C.C. Br). Jchn Taylor, Earlestown ; c.v.T. Sister Mrs. Nurse, Warrington ; C.E. Supt. Bro. J. Lindow, Preecot ; C,.S.J.T. Bro. A. Francis, St. Helens ; C. Sec. Bro. E. Newman, Earlestown ; C. Treas. Bro.:George Tomlin, Warrington ; C. Chap. Bro. J. Hatton, Earlestown, &c. A special vote of sympathy and condolence with Bro. and Sister Singleton, of Earlestown was adopted, Sister Singleton having recently, on one day, lost both parents. wa decided to hold the next session at Eeriest* wn on Saturday, September 14th, when the question of " How Ito improve our Lodges" is to be introduced by Bro. E. Newman, C. Sec., in a short paper, to be followed by a discussion. Thanks were tendered to retiring officers and Bro • George Clarke, P.C.C., responded. Thanks were also accorded the Hindley friends for their excellent arrangements for the session, which was brought to a close at 6 p.m. A public tea followed, and was tar gely attended.

QUARREL BETWEEN WARRINGTON

IRON WORKERS.

PROCEEDINGS AT THE COUNTY COURT.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, an action was heard in which John Pigeon, 112, Liverpool-road, sued John Elwell, 225, Liverpool-road, f3r £lO damages for an assault alleged to have been committed by the former upon the latter at Monks, Hall and Co.'s ironworks, on July sth. Mr. Fowler prosecuted, aud Mr. Arthur Browne defended.

Mr. Fowler said that both plaintiff and defendant worked at Monks, Hall and Co.'s works, the former being a "turner in," and the latter a furnace man. On July sth, the plaintiff and defendant were working on the night shift. Plaintiff's duty was to get the red hot iron through the rolls, where it was rolled into sheets. The defendant's duty was to heat the iron red hot in the furnace and then drag it along the ground by means of tongs to the rolls. On the time in question, the plaintiff complained that the iron was not hot enough. The cause of the complaint was that if the iron was not hot enough, there was great danger of anyone working it,as it was liable to split, and fly about the place. Some words ensued and tlae defendant then put tie tongs round the neck of the plaintiff. They were very hot at the time and burnt the plaintiff very severely. The plaintiff had to be attended to by Dr. Nuttall, and was away from his work three weeks in consequence of the injury he had received. The case had not been taken to the police court because it could be much more ade- quately dealt with at the county court.lt might be argued that there was provocation in the case, hut he (Mr. Fowler) contended that even if there were provocation, there was no excuse for the use of dangerous implements. Mr. Browne said that he was going to say that the defendant acted as he did in self defence. Mr. FoWler contended that even in self defence there was no right for dangerous implements to be used. John Pigeon, the plaintiff, said that he worked at Monks, Hall and Cs. On Friday morning July 5 at about two o'clock. he was working at the stand rolls, rolling iron rods. Defendant was working 10 or 15 yards away and was engaged bringing red hot iron to the rolls, by means of tongs. He had a few words with the defend int about the state of the iron which he said was not hot. Defendant told hum he was a liar and did not know whether the iron was hot or cold. As defendant was going back, he forced the tongs into witness's neck which caused him to fall down. Witness then went to the lodge to have his neck bandaged up. As witness was on the way back, he struck at the defendant with a pair of tongs as he was mad with the pain. Afterwards witness went to Dr. Nuttall and was attended by him. He lost three weeks work and a day and a quarter,those losing £3 12s. In wages. Cross examined : On the night in question he was a turner in. It was the duty of the bolter down to take the iron from the defendant. It was not simply the duty of the bolter down, to complain. Lewis Thomas was the bolter down, and passed the iron to witness. Witness did not run the defendant, and defen- dant did not stand with his back to some railings with the tongs in his hands. There was a large hole about ten feet deep about three yards away from where witness was hurt. Witness did not attempt to push the defen- dant into the hole, and it was not in trying to save him- self that the defendant caught the witness with the tongs. witness did not bri mg the case int, the police. court because he was advised not to do. Witness claimed £lO for damages, three days after the accident. Dr. Nuttall said that he was surgeon at Monks Hall and Co. Ile saw defendant on July sth at about noon at his surgery. He was suffering from burn 4 on each side of the neck. They were not dangerous wounds, but were superficial burns likely to incapscitate him a week or ten days —Cross-examined : Plaintiff told wit- ness that the burns were the result of an accident.

Mr. Browne submitted that the case was an accident brought about by the plaintiff himself. When the de- fendant took the iron to the rolls, the plaintiff had no right to interfere, as it was the duty of the bolter down to do so. On the night in question, some words ensued between plaiptiff and defendant about the iron, and plaintiff ran at defendant in a temper, and defendant backed away until he came to a hole ten feet deep which was railed round to the height of three feet. In this hole there was a quantity of belting, to work the rolls and it was driven ny an engino of 80 or 90 horse power. Had he fallen into thiS hole, the defendant would have been knocked into a thousand pieces. The plaintiff was running at him,and to defend himself,the defendant put up his tongs and plaintiff ran into them. He contend2d that the defendant was not to blame for the accident, as plaintiff had contributed to it by his action. John Elwell, the defendant, corroborated the state- ment of his solicitor.

Lewis Thomas, bolter down, 13 Bramhall-st., said that if there was any compl4 int about the iron, it should come from him or the roller. After the defendant had brought the red hot iron, he was on his way back to the furnace, when the plaintiff threw his tomgs down and ran towards the defendant. The latter backed to the rail- ings round the pit, and put up his tongs to defend him- self from a blow from Pigeou. Pigeon ran against the tongs. The belting wits going round at the time of the quarrel. William Howell, Mersey-st., turner in, said that he was with the gang at the works on the night in question. His evidence was of a similar nature to that of the last witness.

Mr. Fowler sqid that the two last witnesses had been to his office soon after the dispute, and had given de- positions to him in favour of the plaintiff. fie had not enbpceosed them because they promised to come. They had since been subrcenaed by the other side. His Honour in giving judgment,siid that the evidence showed that Pigeon was going to assault Elwell, and the latter was justified in attempting to defend himself. He would give judgment t )r the delendaut.

HARD ON THE DEFENDANT.

On Thursday7at--tht;--iiii—rrin-g—t;Tn—bOunty Court, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, Williani Longshaw, Regent-street, Warrington, draper, sued Mr. Walter smith, gardener. Grappenhall, for £2, the balance owing for drapery supplied.—Mr. Browne appeared for the plaintiff and explained to his Honourlhat the defendant did not dispute that the money was originally owing, but he had said that he had paid a person who was formerly in the employ of the plaintiff. Mr. Browne contended that under the circumstances the onus „Orobandi lay upon the defendant who should prove that he had made the payments. His Honour agreed, and the defendant said he paid to the person referred to the full amount and produced receipts.—Cross- examined: lie admitted that Mr. Longshaw, the plaintiff, saw him and told him that he must not pay any more money to his former employe. The following day, however, he did pay her a sovereign. His Honour told the defendant that it I was unfortunate that he had paid the ..C1 to a per- son whose agency had been revoked according to his admisAon. His Honour then asked if there was anything behind this case and Mr. Browne replied that there was, and his Honour told the defendant that he would hive to pay the .E 1 over spin, and that he would have his remedy against the person he paid the sovereign to. Mr. 'Browne said the plaintiff's sympathies were with the defendant Out he was compelled to stand upon his legal rights. WRINGING IRACHINS GiAncLvs, /mammies Warrington. •

EA RLESI OWN,

EXTENSIVE THEFT OF BRASS AT

At the Warrington Occasional Court on Tuesday, be- fore Mr. James Tinsley, William Trigg was charged with stealing between June Ist and August 3rd, 1741b5. of brass, value £3 10s., the property of the Sankey Sugar Works Earlesto wn

Alfred Ridley, foreman fitter of the Sankey Sugar Works, Earlestown, said that he knew the prisoner who came under his charge on February 16th, as gasmaker. He remained there until Friday, August 2nd, whorl be asked for a day off saying that he had some buslneu to do. The next time witness saw the prisoaer was at, the Warrington Borough police stati„n on August sth. The new and scrap braes produced in court were the pro- perty of the Sankey Sugar Works, and had been kept in the storar om. Several pieces of brass were missed at intervals. c ,mmencing in the beginning of June. Aoout a foitnight after missing the articles, witness In"ked up all the new braes. No one had any right o take any of the things away without squinting witness ot the tact. The value ot the article taken were abotr £3 10s. Fri. goner worked near t) the store room, but he never re- quired any of the articles produced, although he had to go to the stores for white lead. The priaJner always worked satisfactorily. James Toole, marine store dealer. Allen-street, War- rington, said that on August 3rd, rise i r came into his yard, with two bags ot brass in a hand cart. Witness asked who had sent it, and he replied that it was his own, as he had done a bit in tt e brokering line. He asked 3d. a lb. f r it. W,tness offered him 2/d. for it which he agreed to take, but witLe3s could not pay for it until an hour after, and prisoner went aW ty saying that he would return for the money. Witness clever saw the prisoner again until he saw him at the police station on August sth. _ .

Detective-Sergeant Monaghan said that about ten o'clock in the morning of August 3rd, he went to Toole's yard, and there saw Toole and the b as. oroduced. About 10 minutes later, he saw prisoner COining, along Allen-street towards Toole's yard. Witness tofu wed the prisoner into the yard and he stood close to the brass. Witness asked him if he had brought the brass there and he replied he had. Witness told him he was a police officer and wanted to know how he had come into pos- session of it. He said that he had bought it at sales a bit at a time. Witness was not satisfied, and took him to the police station, at the sitTle time taking the brass also. At the police station, the prisoner gave the name of William Williams, 21, Viaduct-strect,Earlestown. He was then charged with being in possession of the brass without giving.a satisfactory account of it. At about 1 p.m. the same day, prisoner's wife came to the police officer, and she saw the prisoner,and said that they lived at Short-street, Newton Common. On August sth, prisoner was brought before the Borough police,and was handed over to P.S. Bankes. P.S. Bankes, stationed at Newton, said that on Mon; day, August sth, he received the prisoner into custody from Detective-Sergeant Monaghan, and conveyed him o the Earlestown Police Station, and charged him with stealing the brass from the Sankey Sugar Works. He replied that he did not value it at £3 103., he was not getting £2 for it. Prisoner after being cautioned said that he was guilty and acknowledged it was takes. He asked to be dealt with in that Court.

Prisoner was committed to the Quarter Sessions for trial.

FRODSHAM.

COUNTY COUNCIL VALUER.—The Cheshire County Council has appointed Mr. Charles E. Linaker, F. 5.1., a referee under the Finance Act of 1894. ODDFELLOWS AT CHURCH. —Over one hundred mem- bers of the Court No. 516 of Oddfellows attended the Parish church on Sunday morning. They proceeded there in processional nrder,and were headed by the Frcd- sham Old Band, which was under the conductorship of Mr. William Jones. The preacher was the Rev. E. Chorley, vicar of Inc. Txcarricar. INSTRUCTION CL ASSES —A meeting of the Frodshain Technical lestruction Committee wa, held in the Board-room, on Monday evening, when there wera present—Messrs. Charles E. LMaker (chairman), J. M. Williams E. Rhodes, T. E alum, H.E. Heywood, and T. Arthur Rigby. From the report of the Secretary (Mr. Sharp) it appear,d th.it the successful pupils who had received instruction in the several classes of the com- mittee were the following :—Geometrical drawing : Mr. Arthur F. Illidge, passed ; five pupils examined. 2b freehand drawing, elementary stage : Fourteen ex- amined. Miss Annie M. Wilkinson, let class ; Misscl D. Robinson, Hilda Rogers, C.E. Harrison' and Mr.Ed ward Earlarn, 2nd class, 3a model drawing, elementary st ige Ten examined. Mr. J. F. Wright, Ist class ; Miss C. E. Harrison and Mr. Howard Earlam, 2ad class. Die committee expressed their satisfaction at the results obtained by the examinations. On the proposition of Mr. Williams, seconded by Mr. Heywood, it was resolved that the technical instruction lessons be continued for another session. The Secretary was authorised to apply to the Chcshire County Council, through Mr. Ward, for a grant of £65 for car- rying out the several classes, :which in all probability would embrace, if found necessary, the under-mentioned subjects :—Free-hand and model drawing, carpentry and geometrical drawing, laundry. dressmaking or cookery. St. John Ambulance, shorthand, typewriting, or bookkeeping.

ACTION FOR MAINTENANC-E.-SEQU'EL TO A POLICE COURT CASE.

Thomas Solan, coachman, was sued by his father- in-law, Thomas Stone,Winwick-st., at the Warring- ton County Court before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes,lor 93.,as 13 weeks'maintenance at 13s. per week. Mr. Forshaw represented the plaintiff and Mr. Browne the defendant. If r. Forshaw said that defendant f irmerfy lodged with the plaintiff, and agreed to pay 133. per week. He owed 13 weeks from 22nd of Slay. 1891, to 15th September of the same year. The plaintiff and his wife gave evidence in support of this statement, and in cross- examination the plaintiff admitted that he only commenced these proceedings on the afternoon of the day he was bound over at the police court at the instance of the defendant for having threatened to stab him with a knife. Mr. Browne contended that the claim would never have been heard of but for the police court proceedings, and suggested that it was only brought forward in consequence of the plaintiff losing his case in the police court.—Both derendant and his wife, who was the daughter of plaintiff, swore that they owed nothing, and that the 'amount which the defendant and plaintiff agreed upon was lls, per week.-4is Honour said he should give a verniet for the de- fendant inasmuch as it was quite clear that the case was brought out of revenge for the police court proceedings. Mr. Forshaw said that he had only been instructed the previous day, otherwise he would have advised that it was not a case to bring into the court,as it was only a family matter, and ought to have been settled outside.

SHIP CANAL NEW STEAMERS FOR THE CLAN LINE. Another of the lines sailing from Manchester is taking advantage of the present cheapness ot materials to make additions to its ti-et. Messrs. Cayzer, Irvine, and Co., owners of the Clan line, have, it is announced, placed contracts with the Naval Construction and Armaments Company, of Harruw,for two large new :.3rPCV steamers, to be named the Clan Menzies and the Clan Lindsay. The new ships will be similar in dimensions and arrange- ment to the Clan Ross. Owing to the quietness of the shipping trade to Bombay this year only the smaller vessels of the Clan line are at present engaged on that service,the largest Clan boat which has yet been to Manchester being the Clan Drummond (300 feet long, 40 feet broad,and 2,920 gross tonnage), which has made two voyages front the canal. All the rest have been steamers of the smaller type,about 305 feet long and a couple of thousand tons gross register. The next boat —the Clan Murray—is smaller still, being 290 feet long and 36 feet broad. The larger boats of the fleet are at present employed in the Calcutta and Sauth and East African trades ; but so soon as the volume of exports to Bombay increases suffiuiently, larger steamers will,in all probability, he placed on that service. CASUALTY IN THE CANAL. The Owentlian Thomas (s), from Iluelva, for Man- chester with pyrites, collided with the barques Flora and Odin, moored along,ide Saltport jetty, on Wednesday morning. All three sustained damage. Two barges lying alongside the barques were also injured.

DRUNK AT NEWTON FAIR. On Wednesday morning at the Town Hall, New- ton, before Mr. C. B. F. Borron, Peter Gallagher, butcher, of Wigan, was charged with being drunk in High-st., Newton, the previous night. He was driving about High-st. with a horse, of which he had charge, to the danger of the public. He was locked up, and fined 103. and costs.
